Computing Connection Matrices of Conley Complexes via Algebraic Morse Theory
Álvaro Torras-Casas, Ka Man Yim, Ulrich Pennig

Abstract
. Given a poset-graded chain complex of vector spaces, a Conley complex is the minimal chain-homotopic reduction of the initial complex that respects the poset grading. A connection matrix is a matrix representing the differential of the Conley complex. In this work, we give an algebraic derivation of the Conley complex and its connection matrix using homological perturbation theory and algebraic Morse theory. Under this framework, we use homology decompositions of relative chain complexes to determine the connection matrix, rather than Forman’s acyclic partial matching in the usual discrete Morse theory setting. This homology decomposition is obtained by means of the clearing optimisation, a commonly used technique in persistent homology. Finally, we show how this algebraic perspective yields an algorithm for computing the connection matrix via column reductions on the differential of the initial complex.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
37B30, 55N31, 55U15, 57Q70

1.
Introduction

Morse theory is the study of homological data compression via gradient flows. In classical Morse theory, the singular homology of a compact manifold can be recovered from a
Morse complex
, a chain complex generated by critical points of a Morse function, and whose boundary operator expresses relations between critical points by gradient flow.
In discrete Morse theory
[
15
]
, Forman transplants smooth Morse theory to the setting of finite CW complexes, where the role of the gradient is enacted by an
acyclic partial matching
of cells.
Given an acyclic partial matching, we can construct a smaller chain complex chain homotopic to the cellular chain complex, with chains generated by only unmatched ‘critical’ cells.

Discrete Morse theory can be further abstracted algebraically as a chain homotopic reduction of
poset-graded chain complexes
, whose links with Morse theory emerged from
Conley theory
, an extension of Morse-theoretic constructs to non-gradient dynamical systems
[
16
,
36
]
.
Given a poset-graded chain complex
(C,d)
of vector spaces, its
Conley complex

(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})~
[
22
, Def. 4.23]
is the
minimal
chain homotopic reduction that respects the poset grading (see
Remark

2.11
), satisfying the following conditions given in
Definition

2.10
:

(1)

There is a chain homotopy equivalence between
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
and
(C,d)
that is compatible with the poset filtration; and

(2)

Restricted within each poset grade, the differential
\widebar{d}
is trivial.

A matrix representation
\Delta
of the differential
\widebar{d}
is called a
connection matrix
.
The chain groups of the Conley complex, called Conley indices, consist of relative homology cycles of
C
supported at each poset grade, generalising the Morse indices associated to critical points (cells) in smooth (discrete) Morse theory. We can consider a Conley complex to be the minimal representative of its equivalence class of complexes in the homotopy category of poset-graded chain complexes.

In this study, we focus on establishing an algebraic derivation of the Conley complex and an associated algorithm for computing the connection matrix
\Delta
. Our main technique is based on the algebraic Morse theory of Sköldberg for complexes with fixed bases, or
based complexes

[
39
,
40
]
. Generalising Forman’s acyclic matching of cells, Sköldberg introduced the notion of ‘Morse matching’ for based complexes, which reproduces discrete Morse theory on an algebraic level. Using homological perturbation theory introduced by
[
5
,
20
]
, we show how a Morse matching fashioned out of
homology decompositions
(
eq.

3
) of relative chain complexes enables us to reduce a poset-graded chain complex to its Conley complex. This derivation, which is our main algebraic contribution, is expressed in the theorem below (we recall a poset is well-founded if there are no infinite descending sequences of elements in
P
, e.g. when
P
is finite).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(C,d)
be a
P
-graded chain complex of vector spaces where
P
is well-founded.
A homology decomposition of the relative chains
C^{p}
of
(C,d)
at each poset grade
\zeta^{p}_{n}:C^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n-1}
induces
a
P
-filtered contraction of
(C,d)
to a Conley complex
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})

(1)

where
\widebar{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}H_{n}^{p}
, and
\widebar{d}=\alpha\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}\beta
for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
constructed from
d
and
\zeta^{p}
in
eqs.

18
,
21
and
22
.

Following the algebraic description of a connection matrix, we show how a homology decomposition of relative chains can be used to construct a four step algorithm for computing the connection matrix, given a matrix representation of
d
(see
Section

4.3
). Since our algebraic description is based on a choice of splitting, we first use the clearing optimisation routine of
[
6
,
2
]
to express the differential of the chain complex in a
separating basis
, which effects the homology decomposition.
After bringing the differential into the separating basis, and after (optionally) eliminating some columns and rows,
we can then perform column reductions on the matrix to produce the connection matrix. The final step then consists of eliminating the redundant columns and rows to isolate the Conley indices.
We prove the correctness of our algorithm in
Proposition

4.11
.

Related Work

We recommend
[
32
]
for a general introduction to Conley index theory
[
7
]
, and
[
33
]
for a contemporary account of Conley complexes in the context of combinatorial dynamics. The concept of a connection matrix in Conley theory was first introduced in
[
16
]
. This topic was further explored and applied in works such as
[
18
,
35
,
36
,
17
]
. Recently
[
22
]
established categorical equivalences between their version of a connection matrix and those of
[
16
]
and
[
36
]
, and showed that their definition of a connection matrix, which we follow, is unique up to a choice of
P
-filtered basis. In
[
36
,
33
]
, they showed the existence Conley complexes by implicitly constructing it by induction.

We refer the reader to
[
34
]
for comprehensive guide to smooth Morse theory and its applications and extensions in other areas of pure mathematics, such as
[
44
,
14
]
. A classic summary of the contributions of Morse theory to other areas of mathematics was written by
[
4
]
. For texts on discrete Morse theory, the reader can consult
[
27
]
, or
[
25
,
38
]
which describe both smooth and discrete aspects. Alongside Sköldberg’s algebraic generalisation of Morse theory, we also direct the reader to
[
26
]
for a similar approach. We note that the algebraic Morse theory of
[
39
]

has also been applied to other computational topology problems as well, such as signal compression on simplicial complexes
[
13
]
, and the computation of cellular sheaf cohomology
[
8
]
.

Until recently, the focus of computing the connection matrix is in the setting where the initial chain complex is derived from a cellular complex, where Forman’s discrete gradients (acyclic partial matchings)
[
15
]
can be leveraged in the computation
[
31
,
21
,
23
,
22
]
. Algorithms that are purely algebraic, without reliance on any discrete gradients, have been recently proposed by
[
11
,
9
]
. The computation of an optimal acyclic partial matching that minimises the critical cells is
NP
-hard, equivalent to solving an integer programming problem
[
24
]
. In
[
22
,
23
]
, the authors showed that efficient heuristics for computing non-optimal matchings, such as the
MorseReduce
algorithm
[
31
]
, can be incorporated in a recursive routine to compute a connection matrix without directly solving the integer programming problem.

Our contribution

In this work, we show that choosing homology decompositions at each poset grade, one obtains the chain contraction from a poset-graded complex to its Conley complex by directly applying algebraic Morse theory from
[
40
]
.
Furthermore, we not only present an algebraic derivation of the Conley complex, but also translate the algebraic derivation into an algorithm for computing the connection matrix, following a line of recent work by
[
22
,
11
,
33
]
. The development of the algorithm is enabled by the explicit description of the Conley complex in terms of algebraic formulae, a consequence of applying homological perturbation theory to homology decompositions.
A benefit of our approach is that we obtain an explicit expression for the entries of the connection matrix for a given choice of separating bases, see
Corollary

4.9
.

Our algorithm for computing the connection matrix is similar to that of the reduction algorithms of
[
11
,
9
]
; like theirs, our approach relies essentially on Gaussian elimination to compute the Conley complex in the general algebraic setting, and has complexity
O(N^{3})
, where
N
is the dimension (size of the basis) of the chain complex.
Our approach differs in that we express the connection matrix explicitly in a relative homology basis, and the homology decomposition is explicitly computed (see
Algorithm

1
). On the other hand,
[
11
,
9
]
implicitly infer the homology decomposition of relative chains in their routine, and the connection matrix obtained is
P
-filtered equivalent to the one we obtain in the relative homology basis.

Organisation

In
Section

2
, we introduce the main concepts and theoretical background material for this article. In
Section

2.1
, we set out the relevant concepts relating to chain complexes, namely contractions, homology decompositions, and the homological perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
). In
Section

2.2
, we define poset-graded chain complexes, and the Conley complex. Last but not least, we give an account of Sköldberg’s algebraic Morse theory in
Section

2.3
, and introduce based complexes and Morse matchings.

We prove our main algebraic result,
Theorem

1.1
, in
Section

3
.
Section

3.1
gives the proof purely from the perspective of homological perturbation theory. In
Section

3.2
, we show how we can arrive at the same derivation of the Conley complex by transforming the
P
-graded complex into a based complex. Readers more familiar with discrete Morse theory may find the derivation in
Section

3.2
more congenial as it generalises concepts in discrete Morse theory; for those more focused on homological algebra, the proof in
Section

3.1
is self-contained, and we do not rely on concepts introduced in
Sections

2.3
and
3.2
for the proof or subsequent sections.

Our second main result is an algorithm to compute the connection matrix described in
Section

4
.
We start this section by showing that clearing optimisation
[
6
,
2
]
leads to a homology decomposition of chain complexes over a field as well as to a separating basis associated to it; see
Definition

4.2
.
The algorithm then follows the steps outlined in
Section

4.3
and a pseudocode instantiation is given in
Algorithm

3
.
We conclude this section with the proof of
Proposition

4.11
, which shows that the result of the algorithm is indeed a connection matrix.

A conclusion and a discussion on future research directions can be found in
Section

5
.

2.
Background

In this section, we introduce the necessary background concepts for the article. We first review standard definitions of chain complex and contractions, and we detail relevant properties of
P
-graded chain complexes. In
Section

2.2
, we introduce the Conley complex. At the end, we briefly review algebraic Morse theory from the point of view of the perturbation lemma.

2.1.
Chain Complexes over a Field

We consider a
chain complex

(C,d)
of vector spaces
C=\{C_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
over some field, together with
differentials

d
, which are linear maps
d_{n}\colon C_{n}\rightarrow C_{n-1}
satisfying
d_{n-1}d_{n}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We always write
C
to refer to a chain complex
(C,d)
, dropping the explicit mention of the differential.
Given
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
C[r]
for the
r
-shifted chain complex given by
C[r]_{n}=C_{n+r}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
; the differential is also shifted similarly.
Given two chain complexes,
(C,d^{C})
and
(D,d^{D})
, a map of degree
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
g\colon C\rightarrow D[r]
, is a collection of linear maps
g_{n}\colon C_{n}\rightarrow D_{n+r}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this context, a map
f\colon C\rightarrow D[r]
is a
chain map
if the collection of linear maps
f_{n}\colon C_{n}\rightarrow D_{n+r}
is such that
d^{D}_{n+r}f_{n}=f_{n-1}d^{C}_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. A chain complex
D
is a
subcomplex
of another chain complex
C
if
D_{n}\subset C_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and the differentials of
D
are restrictions of the ones of
C
.

2.1.1.
Homology Decomposition

Let
C
be a chain complex.
The kernel and image of the differential
d
, denoted by
\ker(d)
and
\mathrm{im}(d)
respectively, are subcomplexes of
C
.
Recall
\ker(d_{n})
and
\mathrm{im}(d_{n+1})
are called the
n
-cycles and
n
-boundaries of
C_{n}
, and the
homology
group is given by the quotients

H_{n}(C)=\ker(d_{n})\big/\mathrm{im}(d_{n+1})
.
For each dimension
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can choose to decompose the chain groups as a direct sum

(2)

\zeta:C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\ ,

where we use the notation
B_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{im}(d_{n+1})
, and
K_{n}\coloneqq B_{n-1}
.
The decomposition is induced by considering the following two short exact sequences of vector spaces; the first

\hbox to273.69pt{\vbox to19.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 136.84265pt\lower-8.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-136.84265pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -189.35 -3.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

allows us to choose a splitting
C_{n}\cong Z_{n}\oplus K_{n}
, where

Z_{n}\coloneqq\ker(d_{n})
.
We have another splitting
Z_{n}\cong H_{n}\oplus B_{n}
obtained from the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{im}(d_{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
.

An arbitrary set of isomorphisms
\{\zeta_{n}\colon C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
need not be mutually compatible across chain groups of different dimensions. The set of isomorphisms
\{\zeta_{n}\}
is said to be a
homology decomposition (of
C
)
(c.f.
[
33
, Prop 3.4.5.]
) if the following diagram commutes for all
n
:

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n-1}\oplus B_{n-1}\oplus K_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The isomorphisms involved in splitting the short exact sequences do satisfy the consistency requirement.
The existence of homology decompositions is a standard result which follows from
C
being a chain complex over a field; see for example the introduction to chapter 1.4. from
[
43
]
or Proposition 3.4.5 from
[
33
]
.
In
Section

4.1
, we study how to compute such decompositions in practice.

Remark 2.1
.

Notice that the splitting in
eq.

2
is
unnatural
.
This means that, given a chain map
f\colon C\rightarrow D
, the morphism
f_{n}
is not the direct sum of three chain maps between the subcomplexes
H,B,K
of
C
and
D
.

Example 2.2
.

In this example, we illustrate how a set of isomorphisms
\{\zeta_{n}\colon C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
do not necessarily lead to a homology decomposition.
Consider the simplicial complex
[0,1]
giving the unit interval with
0
-simplices
\{0\}
and
\{1\}
and
1
-simplex
[0,1]
. Its nontrivial chain groups over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are
C_{1}=\langle[0,1]\rangle
and
C_{0}=\langle[0],[1]\rangle
, while its nontrivial subcomplexes of homology, boundaries and preboundaries are
B_{0}=K_{1}=\langle[0]+[1]\rangle
and
H_{0}=C_{0}/B_{0}
.
Then, consider a pair of isomorphisms
\zeta_{1}\colon C_{1}\rightarrow K_{1}
, given by the assignment
[0,1]\mapsto[0]+[1]
, and
\zeta_{0}\colon C_{0}\rightarrow H_{0}\oplus B_{0}
, given by the assignments
[0]\mapsto([0]+B_{0},0)
and
[1]\mapsto(0,[0]+[1])
.
In this case, one can check that
\zeta_{0}(d_{1}(\zeta_{1}^{-1}(K_{1})))=([0]+B_{0},[0]+[1])
, which is not contained in
B_{0}
. Hence,
\{\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1}\}
is not a homology decomposition.
On the other hand, changing
\zeta_{0}
for
\zeta^{\prime}_{0}\colon C_{0}\rightarrow H_{0}\oplus B_{0}
, given by the assignment
[0]\mapsto([0]+B_{0},0)
and
[0]+[1]\mapsto(0,[0]+[1])
, leads to a homology decomposition
\{\zeta_{0}^{\prime},\zeta_{1}\}
.

2.1.2.
Chain Homotopy and Contractions

A chain homotopy equivalence between two chain complexes
(C,d)
and
(C^{\prime},d^{\prime})
consists of chain maps
f,g
, and a collection of maps
h_{n}:C_{n}\to C_{n+1}
and
k_{n}:C^{\prime}_{n}\to C^{\prime}_{n+1}
,

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{n}:\ C_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{n}:\ H_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{n}:\ C_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{n}=:K_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n+1}\oplus B_{n+1}\oplus K_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{n+1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n+1}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}

The example above shows how a homology decomposition reduces a chain complex to a minimal homotopy equivalent complex, composed of homology groups with trivial differentials.
Our work considers the generalisation of this elementary result to poset-graded chain complexes, which we introduce below.
Our main result,
Theorem

1.1
, describes how homology decompositions of
relative
chains induce a contraction on the total complex that is compatible with the poset-grading.
The smaller complex resulting from the contraction is precisely the Conley complex, which we introduce in
Definition

2.10
. We first introduce the main device with which we prove
Theorem

1.1
, which is the homological perturbation lemma of Brown and Gugenheim
[
5
,
20
]
.

2.1.3.
Homological Perturbation

Homological perturbation allows us to derive contractions of chain complexes by perturbing known contractions of other complexes.
A
perturbation

\delta
of a chain complex
(A,d)
is a collection of degree minus one maps
\delta_{n}:A_{n}\to A_{n-1}
such that
(d+\delta)^{2}=0
. We call
(A,d+\delta)
the perturbed complex. Given a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
, our aim is to build a trivial contraction of a proxy complex
(A,d_{A})
, and a perturbation
\delta
such that
(C,d)=(A,d_{A}+\delta)
. We can then apply the following perturbation lemma to obtain a contraction of
(C,d)
.

For the lemma below, we recall a linear transformation
f:V\to W
is locally nilpotent if for any
v\in V
, there is a finite
n
such that
f^{n}(v)=0
.

If a perturbation
\delta
of
C
is such that the linear map
h\delta:\bigoplus_{n}C_{n}\to\bigoplus_{n}C_{n}
is locally nilpotent, then we can obtain a contraction of the perturbed complex
(C,d+\delta)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C^{\prime},d^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(C,d+\delta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}

where, writing
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-h\delta)^{-1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\delta(h\delta)^{i}
, the induced maps are given by

(8)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f\mathcal{S}g,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=f+f\mathcal{S}h,

\displaystyle g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=g+h\mathcal{S}g,

\displaystyle h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=h+h\mathcal{S}h.

Remark 2.5
.

We note that, in the original presentation of the perturbation lemma
[
5
,
20
]
, the chain complexes are required to admit a lower bounded exhaustive filtration; the contraction
(f,g,h)
need to be compatible with the filtration; and the perturbation
\delta
must be filtration lowering. These assumptions exist to ensure the sum in
\mathcal{S}
is well-defined. Here we subsume this by requiring
h\delta
to be locally nilpotent.

2.2.
Poset-graded Chain Complexes

In this section, we follow the framework of
[
36
,
22
,
33
]
. Let
(P,\leq)
be a poset.
Henceforth, we impose the following finiteness assumption on the poset
P
.
We say that a partial order on a set
P
is
well-founded
if there is no strictly descending infinite sequence in
P
.
That is, for any descending sequence
p_{1}\geq p_{2}\geq\cdots
, we have that
p_{k}=p_{k+1}
for sufficiently large
k
.

A vector space
V
is
P
-graded if it admits a decomposition into a direct sum
V=\bigoplus_{p\in P}V^{p}
indexed by poset elements.
For
p\in P
, we let
\imath^{p}:V^{p}\hookrightarrow V
denote the natural inclusion, and
\jmath^{p}:V\twoheadrightarrow V^{p}
denote the projection
(v_{q})_{q\in P}\mapsto v_{p}
.
In particular, we note that
\mathrm{id}_{V}=\sum_{p}\imath^{p}\circ\jmath^{p}
.

For a linear map
f:V\to W
between
P
-graded vector spaces
V=\bigoplus_{p\in P}V^{p}
and
W=\bigoplus_{p\in P}W^{p}
, we denote by
f^{pq}
the composition

We say
f
is
P
-filtered
if
f^{pq}\neq 0\implies p\leq q
. Composition of
P
-filtered linear maps are also
P
-filtered
[
22
, Prop 4.3]
: if
f:V\to W
and
g:W\to U
are
P
-filtered, then

(9)

\displaystyle gf=\sum_{p\leq q}(gf)^{pq}\quad\text{and}\quad(gf)^{pq}=\sum_{p\leq r\leq q}g^{pr}f^{rq}.

In particular,
(gf)^{pp}=g^{pp}f^{pp}
.
A chain complex
(C,d)
is
P
-graded
if the chain group at each dimension
C_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}C^{p}_{n}
is
P
-graded, and the differentials
d_{n}:C_{n}\to C_{n-1}
are
P
-filtered. For any
p\in P
, we let
F^{p}_{n}=\bigoplus_{r\leq p}C^{r}_{n}
, and
F^{\widecheck{p}}_{n}=\bigoplus_{r\lneq p}C^{r}_{n}
denote the sublevel set and strict sublevel set of the
P
-graded chain complex at
p
. Since
d_{n}
are
P
-filtered, and
F^{p}_{n}
and
F^{\widecheck{p}}_{n}
are downward closed with respect to
\leq
, the restrictions of the total complex to
F^{p}
and
F^{\widecheck{p}}
are also
P
-graded chain complexes.

We say a chain map
f:C\to D
between
P
-graded chain complexes is
P
-filtered if for each dimension
f_{n}:C_{n}\to D_{n}
are
P
-filtered linear maps. We say an equivalence of
P
-graded chain complexes as in
eq.

4
is
P
-filtered if linear maps
h_{n},k_{n}
and chain maps
f,g
are
P
-filtered. In particular, we say a contraction
(f,g,h)
in the form of
eq.

6
is
P
-filtered if
f,g,h
are all
P
-filtered.

Remark 2.6
.

If
P
is finite, then Birkhoff’s representation theorem (
[
3
]
, or
[
37
, Theorem 6.2,10.4]
) relates the poset with the finite distributive lattice
\mathsf{O}(P)
of downsets of
P
. Recall a subset
A\subset P
is a downset if it is downward closed: for any
q\in A
, if
p\leq q
, then
p\in A
. For each
p\in P
, we can send it to the corresponding downset
\downarrow p:=\{q\in P\ :\ q\leq p\}
. Birkhoff’s representation theorem states that
\{\downarrow p\}_{p\in P}
are precisely the elements of the lattice
\mathsf{O}(P)
which have a unique predecessor in the lattice partial order — the
join-irreducible elements

\mathsf{J}(\mathsf{O}(P))
— and this bijection is a poset isomorphism
P\cong\mathsf{J}(\mathsf{O}(P))
. We note that for
\downarrow p
, the unique predecessor in
\mathsf{O}(P)
is
\downarrow p\setminus\{p\}
.
Considering the lattice of downset
\mathsf{O}(P)
allows us to view the
P
-graded chain complex as a lattice filtered complex, a perspective articulated in
[
22
, §7.2]
and
[
41
, §2]
. An
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered vector space
V
is one equipped with a lattice homomorphism
\mathrm{flt}:\mathsf{O}(P)\to\mathsf{Sub}(V)
, where
\mathsf{Sub}(V)
is the lattice of vector subspaces with join
\vee=+
and meet
\wedge=\cap
.
A
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
is an instance of an
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered chain complex, in the following sense. The chain group in each dimension
C_{n}
is an
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered vector space
\mathrm{flt}_{n}:\mathsf{O}(P)\to\mathsf{Sub}(C_{n})
, where

\mathrm{flt}_{n}(A)=\bigoplus_{p\in A}C^{p}_{n}.

Each differential
d_{n}:C_{n}\to C_{n-1}
is
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered, meaning it satisfies
d_{n}(\mathrm{flt}_{n}(A))\subseteq\mathrm{flt}_{n-1}(A)
for all
A\in\mathsf{O}(P)
. Given this framework, we can then regard the sublevel set chain complexes as lattice filtered subcomplexes of
(C,d)
:

\bigoplus_{n}F^{p}_{n}=\mathrm{flt}(\downarrow p),\quad\bigoplus_{n}F^{\widecheck{p}}_{n}=\mathrm{flt}(\downarrow p\setminus\{p\}).

2.2.1.
Relative Chain Complexes

We define, for
p,q\in P
, the
twisted differentials
to be the linear maps

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{n}^{pq}:C^{q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{p}_{n-1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{p}}

In particular, since
d_{n}
is
P
-filtered, it follows that
t_{n}^{pq}\neq 0
implies
p\leq q
.
We call
(C,t)
a
multicomplex
1
1

1

This is the same notion of a multicomplex in
[
42
,
19
,
29
]
if
P=\mathbb{Z}
.
indexed by
P
. To abbreviate notation we let
t^{p}:=t^{pp}
. Because
d^{2}=0
,
eq.

9
implies the twisted differentials of a multicomplex satisfy the following identity for fixed
p\leq q
:

(11)

\displaystyle\sum_{p\leq r\leq q}t^{pr}_{n-1}t^{rq}_{n}

\displaystyle=(d^{2})^{pq}=0.

In particular,
t^{p}_{n-1}t^{p}_{n}=0
. This implies
(C^{p},t^{p})
are chain complexes in their own right. Because we have a short exact sequence of chain complexes,

Remark 2.7
.

While
t^{pq}_{n}:C^{q}_{n}\to C^{p}_{n-1}
are linear maps between the chain groups, the relations between the twisted differentials in
eq.

11
imply the collection of linear maps
t^{pq}_{n}
do not constitute a chain map from
(C^{q},t^{q})
to
(C^{p},t^{p})
, as they violate the commutativity condition required.
More concretely, generally one has that
t^{pq}_{n-1}\circ t^{q}_{n}\neq t^{p}_{n-1}\circ t^{pq}_{n}
.
For instance, in
Example

2.9
twisted differentials
anti-commute
.

Remark 2.8
.

We also note that a
P
-filtered chain homotopy equivalence
C\simeq D
descends to a chain homotopy equivalence of relative chain complexes
C^{p}\simeq D^{p}
for all
p\in P
. The chain homotopy can be obtained by restricting the
P
-filtered maps
f,g,h,k
in the chain homotopy described in
eq.

4
to the relative chain complexes at each poset level
p
:

which satisfy
t^{01}_{n-1}t^{1}_{n}+t^{0}_{n-1}t^{01}_{n}=0
.
The sublevel sets are
F^{0}=C^{0}
and
F^{1}=C
. The chain complex
(C^{1},t^{1})
is isomorphic to the relative chain complex
F^{1}/F^{0}
. The short exact sequence
F_{n}^{0}\to F_{n}^{1}\to F^{1}_{n}/F^{0}_{n}
gives rise to the long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(C^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n}(C^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{n-1}(C^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}}

We leave it as a standard diagram chasing exercise to the reader that the differential

d_{n}\colon H_{n}(C^{1})\to H_{n-1}(C^{0})

is induced by the twisted differential
t^{01}_{n}
.

2.2.2.
The Conley Complex

We say the differential
d
of a
P
-graded chain complex is
strict
if
t^{p}_{n}=0
for all
p\in P
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that
d
is strict if and only if
C^{p}_{n}\cong H_{n}(C^{p})
. Given a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
, the main subject of our study is the chain homotopic reduction of
(C,d)
to a strict chain complex, which is the
Conley complex
.

Definition 2.10
.

Given a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
, we say another
P
-graded chain complex
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
is a
Conley complex
of
(C,d)
, if:

(M1)

There is a
P
-filtered chain homotopy equivalence
(C,d)\simeq(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
; and

(M2)

\widebar{d}
is strict.

We refer to the chain groups
\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}
of the Conley complex as the
Conley indices
, and we say that a matrix
\Delta
representing
\widebar{d}
is
a
connection matrix
of
(C,d)
.

If a Conley complex exists for a
P
-graded chain complex, then it is unique up to
P
-filtered chain isomorphisms.
This is due to a remarkable fact
[
22
, Proposition 4.27]
about strict complexes: two strict
P
-graded chain complexes are
P
-filtered chain isomorphic, if and only if they are
P
-filtered chain homotopy equivalent.
The existence of Conley complexes for finite
P
-graded chain complexes was first shown in
[
36
, Theorem 8.1, Cor. 8.2]
; we also refer the reader to
[
33
, Lemma 5.3.1, Theorem 5.3.2]
for a more recent account of the proof.

Remark 2.11
.

We can regard the Conley complex
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
of
(C,d)
as the unique
P
-graded homotopy equivalent complex of
(C,d)
which is minimal within every poset grade. The Conley complex replaces the relative chain complex
C^{p}
at each poset grading with its minimal equivalent complex (in the sense presented in
Example

2.3
). The relative chain complexes
(\widebar{C}^{p},\widebar{d}^{p})
at each grading of the Conley complex has trivial differentials
\widebar{d}^{p}=0
(by definition
(M2)
), and relative chain groups

\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}\cong H_{n}(C^{p}).

See
[
36
, §8]
. This follows from the defining properties of the Conley complex: the strictness condition
(M2)
implies
\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}\cong H_{n}(\widebar{C}^{p})
, the
P
-filtered equivalence
(M2)
implies via
Remark

2.8
the equivalence of relative chains
\widebar{C}^{p}\simeq C^{p}
at each poset level. Passing to homology, we thus obtain
\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}\cong H_{n}(C^{p})
.

Before we move on to discussing algebraic Morse theory, we first give a few example applications of
P
-graded chain complexes in mathematics. A key example of a
P
-graded chain complex is one derived from (multi-parameter) filtrations of simplicial complexes, a common occurrence in applied and computational topology.
Let
X
be a finite simplicial complex, and let
(C,d)
be a chain complex associated to
X
.
Viewing
X
as a poset where the simplices are ordered by face relations, a filter function of
X
is a monotone map
f:X\to P
to another poset
P
. Given such a filter function
f
, we can express
(C,d)
as a
P
-graded chain complex, where
C^{p}_{n}
are vector spaces whose basis are the
n
-simplices in
{f}^{-1}\left(p\right)
.
The chain complexes
F^{p}
and
F^{\widecheck{p}}
are then the chain complexes of the subcomplexes
\{f\leq p\}
and
\{f\lneq p\}
respectively.

Example 2.12
.

Let
X=\Delta^{2}
be the simplicial complex shown in
Fig.

1
. The numbers on vertices, edges and the face indicate the values of a filter function
f\colon X\to P
, where
P
is the linear poset
\{0\leq 1\leq 2\leq 3\}
.
The associated chain complex is
P
-graded.
The basis of
C_{1}^{2}
, for example, is given by the two edges (i.e.
1
-simplices) marked by
2
in
Fig.

1
; these are also denoted as
uv
and
vw
using the labelling on the right hand side of
Fig.

1
.

0
1
2
1
2
2
3
u
w
v

Figure 1.
Left: The simplicial complex
X=\Delta^{2}
together with an example of a filtration function
f\colon X\to P
for the linear poset
P=\{0,1,2,3\}
. Right: the vertices of
\Delta^{2}
labelled by
v,u,w
.

The concept of a
P
-graded chain complex comes up in Conley theory for dynamical systems
[
7
,
32
]
, from which we derive terms such as the Conley complex and connection matrix.
Given a dynamical system, and a specified finite lattice of attractors, one can obtain a
Morse decomposition
of the underlying space, a partition indexed over the poset
P
of join-irreducible elements of the lattice
[
36
,
22
]
.
The partial order in the poset encodes attractor-repeller relationships between the attractors in the lattice they index.
The partition
P
then induces a splitting of the total singular chain complex of the domain as a
P
-graded chain complex.
In that context, the connection matrix describes algebraic relations between the Conley indices of the isolated invariant sets associated to the Morse decomposition
[
36
,
32
]
.

Concrete computational examples of such
P
-graded chain complexes from Morse decompositions can be found in combinatorial dynamics
[
1
,
12
,
28
,
11
]
. A
multivector field
on a simplicial complex is a partition of the simplicial complex, where each partition element (multivector) is a convex subset of the face poset of the complex
[
11
, Def 2.2]
. This notion of a combinatorial vector field generalises Forman’s discrete vector field
[
15
]
, defined as a partial matching of cells. The associated multivector field generates a multi-valued function on simplices, and the combinatorial dynamical system is given by iterations of this multi-valued function. The Morse decomposition of the simplicial complex can then be obtained as the condensation of a directed graph associated to the dynamical system. We give a brief illustrative example below but refer the reader to
[
10
, §3.4]
for technical details.

Example 2.13
.

Consider the simplicial complex
X
shown on the left hand side of
Fig.

2
with vertex set
\{A,B,C,D,E\}
. The set

\{\{B,AB\},\{A,AE\},\{E,ED\},\{D,CD\},\{C,BC\},\{AD\},\{AC,ABC\},\{ADE\}\}

is a multivector field on
X
. The partition into Morse sets and the corresponding poset
P
are shown on the right of
Fig.

2
. The dynamics on edges are indicated by the arrows.
The chain complex of
X
is
P
-graded.
The chain group
C_{1}(X)
, for example, is
7
-dimensional with one basis element corresponding to the red edge, one to the blue edge and the remaining five corresponding to the trapezoid.

A
B
C
D
E

Figure 2.
Left: the simplicial complex with the multivector field from Example 2.4. Right: the poset associated to the partition into Morse sets.

2.3.
Algebraic Morse Theory for Based Complexes

We now introduce Sköldberg’s algebraic Morse theory framework
[
39
]
. The main idea is to generalise acyclic partial matchings on cell complexes in discrete Morse theory
[
15
]
to a purely algebraic setting. We first define the based complex, which generalises the notion of cells, and define Morse matchings, which is the algebraic anlogue of acyclic partial matchings. We then give a summary of Sköldberg’s derivation of algebraic Morse theory from homological perturbation theory (
[
40
]
), which concerns how Morse matchings can be used to derive contractions of chain complexes.

2.3.1.
Based Complexes

Definition 2.14
.

A chain complex of vector spaces
(C,d)
is a
based complex
if for each vector space
C_{n}
, there is a direct sum decomposition
C_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I_{n}}C_{n}^{(a)}
as vector spaces, where
I=(I_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a mutually disjoint collection of indexing sets.

Given a based complex
(C,d)
for
a\in I_{n}
, there is an injection
\imath_{n}^{(a)}\colon C_{n}^{(a)}\to C_{n}
and a projection
\jmath_{n}^{(a)}\colon C_{n}\to C_{n}^{(a)}
such that
\jmath_{n}^{(a)}\imath_{n}^{(a)}=\mathrm{id}_{n}^{(a)}
, where
\mathrm{id}_{n}^{(a)}
denotes the identity map on
C_{n}^{(a)}
. For a chain map of degree
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\colon C\to C[r]
, and for
a\in I_{n+r}
and
b\in I_{n}
, we denote by
f^{(ab)}
the following composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}^{(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n+r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n+r}^{(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{n}^{(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath_{n+r}^{(a)}}

In particular, given
a\in I_{n-1}
and
b\in I_{n}
, we consider the restriction of the differential
d^{(ab)}:C^{(b)}_{n}\to C^{(a)}_{n-1}
.

Next, we give a concrete example of a based complex due to a homology decomposition of chain groups.

Example 2.15
.

Let
C
be a chain complex equipped with a homology decomposition
\zeta_{n}:C_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}
. We can express
C
as a based complex by writing the chain groups as

C_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{V\in\{H,B,K\}}C^{(V)}_{n}

where
C^{(V)}_{n}:=V_{n}
for
V\in\{H,B,K\}
. Given the maps
\zeta_{n}
, the projections and inclusions are given by

\displaystyle\jmath_{n}^{(V)}

\displaystyle:C_{n}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\zeta_{n}}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\twoheadrightarrow V_{n}

\displaystyle\imath_{n}^{(V)}

\displaystyle:V_{n}\hookrightarrow H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\xrightarrow[\cong]{(\zeta_{n})^{-1}}C_{n}.

For
V,W\in\{H,B,K\}
, the differentials restricted to each subgroup are

(12)

d_{n}^{(WV)}:V_{n}\xhookrightarrow{\imath^{(V)}_{n}}C_{n}\xrightarrow{d_{n}}C_{n-1}\overset{\jmath^{(W)}_{n}}{\twoheadrightarrow}W_{n}.

As
\zeta
is a homology decomposition,
and since
(\zeta_{n})^{-1}(H_{n}\oplus B_{n})=\ker d_{n}
, the only non-zero map is where
V=K
(i.e. when
d
acts on the pre-boundaries).
Furthermore, the characterisation of homology decompositions in
eq.

3
implies
d_{n}^{(BK)}:K_{n}\to B_{n-1}
is the identity (by definition)
K_{n}:=B_{n-1}
on the right hand side of the commutative diagram, and the only non-zero block for
V,W\in\{H,B,K\}
.

2.3.2.
Morse Matchings

We now introduce the concept of Morse matchings as given in
[
39
]
. Given a based complex
(C,d)
, we denote by
G(C)
a directed graph with vertices indexed over the subspace indices
\bigsqcup_{n}I_{n}
, together with directed edges
b\rightharpoonup a
whenever
d^{(ab)}\neq 0
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
b\in I_{n}
and
a\in I_{n-1}
.
A
partial matching
\mathsf{M}
on a digraph

G
is a subset of directed edges
\mathsf{M}
such that no two edges in
\mathsf{M}
share a common vertex.
We denote
G^{\mathsf{M}}
to be the digraph obtained by reversing the orientation of the directed edges in
\mathsf{M}
. We use
a\rightharpoondown b
to denote an edge in
G^{\mathsf{M}}
which corresponds to
b\rightharpoonup a
in
\mathsf{M}
.

Example 2.16
.

In the case where the based complex is given by a homology decomposition (
Example

2.15
), the digraph
G(C)
has vertices indexed over
I_{n}=\{H_{n},B_{n},K_{n}\}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and directed edges
K_{n}\rightharpoonup B_{n-1}
. We can construct a partial matching
\mathsf{M}
by including all such directed edges whenever
B_{n}\neq 0
. The digraph
G^{\mathsf{M}}
is then the directed graph with directed edges
B_{n-1}\rightharpoondown K_{n}
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
(C,d)
be a based complex.
A partial matching
\mathsf{M}
on
G(C)
is a
Morse matching
if:

(1)

d^{(ab)}:C^{(b)}\to C^{(a)}

is an isomorphism for all

(b\rightharpoonup a)\in\mathsf{M}
; and

(2)

We have a well-founded partial order
\preceq_{n}
on
I_{n}
satisfying the following: for
a\neq b
, if there is a directed path
a\rightharpoonup c\rightharpoondown b
or
a\rightharpoondown c\rightharpoonup b
in
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
, then we have
a\succneq_{n}b
.

If
\mathsf{M}
is a Morse matching, then we say
\mathsf{M}^{0}
is the
critical set
of the matching, which consists of indexing elements in
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}I_{n}
which are neither sources nor targets of any edge in
\mathsf{M}
.

Example 2.18
.

Consider the case where the based complex is given by a homology decomposition (
Example

2.15
), and the partial matching
\mathsf{M}
given in
Example

2.16
where all the directed edges
K_{n}\rightharpoonup B_{n-1}
are included. In this case,
\mathsf{M}
is a Morse matching. We first note that all non-trivial paths in
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
are of length
1
from
B_{n-1}\rightharpoondown K_{n}
, and thus there are no relations between elements of
I_{n}
. The second condition of a Morse matching is thus trivially satisfied for
\mathsf{M}
. Since
d_{n}^{(BK)}
are isomorphisms, the first condition of the Morse matching is also satisfied for
\mathsf{M}
. Because all
K_{n},B_{n}
’s are respectively sources and edges of the Morse matching, the critical set
\mathsf{M}^{0}
is given by the homology groups
\{H_{n}\}
.

We note that
Definition

2.17
does not impose conditions on
G(C)
.
However, when
G(C)
is finite, there is a simple characterisation for Morse matchings.

Lemma 2.19

(
[
39
, Lemma 1]
)
.

Let
(C,d)
be a based complex such that the digraph
G(C)
is finite, and let
\mathsf{M}
be a partial matching on
G(C)
such that
d^{(ab)}
is an isomorphism for all

(b\rightharpoonup a)\in\mathsf{M}
.
Then,
\mathsf{M}
is a
Morse matching
if and only if
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
has no directed cycles.

2.3.3.
Contractions via Morse Matchings

We now recall the main result of
[
39
,
40
]
. By applying the perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
), Sköldberg derives a contraction of a based complex
C
given a Morse matching
\mathsf{M}
. Let us denote by

\widetilde{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathsf{M}^{0}_{n}}C^{(a)}_{n}

the critical subspaces; we let
f_{n}:\widetilde{C}_{n}\leftrightarrows C_{n}:g_{n}
be the natural inclusion and projection maps between the subspace
\widetilde{C}_{n}
and
C_{n}
. We also define
h_{n}:C_{n}\to C_{n+1}
and
\tau_{n}:C_{n}\to C_{n-1}
:

(13)

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{(b\rightharpoonup a)\in\mathsf{M}}\imath_{n+1}^{(b)}\circ(d_{n+1}^{(ab)})^{-1}\circ\jmath_{n}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\tau_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(b\rightharpoonup a)\notin\mathsf{M}}\imath_{n-1}^{(a)}\circ d_{n}^{(ab)}\circ\jmath_{n}^{(b)}.

Since
\mathsf{M}
is a Morse matching, for
b\rightharpoonup a
in
\mathsf{M}
,
d^{(ab)}
is an isomorphism so
h
is well-defined. By perturbing the contraction

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{C},0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C,d-\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

with
\tau
on the right hand side, we can obtain the contraction
eq.

15
in
Theorem

2.20
via the perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
).

Theorem 2.20

(
[
40
, Lemma 2]
)
.

Let
(C,d)
be a based complex where a Morse matching
\mathsf{M}
exists. For
h,\tau
as defined in
eq.

13
, if
h\tau:\bigoplus C_{n}\to\bigoplus C_{n}
is locally nilpotent, then we have a contraction

(15)

where
\widetilde{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathsf{M}^{0}_{n}}C^{(a)}_{n}
, and the induced maps are given by

(16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}

\displaystyle=f\mathcal{S}g,

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}

\displaystyle=f+f\mathcal{S}h,

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}

\displaystyle=g+h\mathcal{S}g,

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}

\displaystyle=h+h\mathcal{S}h.

for
f,g,h,\tau
as defined in
eq.

13
and
\mathcal{S}=\tau(1-h\tau)^{-1}
.

Example 2.21
.

Given a chain complex with a homology decomposition
\zeta
, we recover the contraction of the chain complex in
Example

2.3
if we ‘apply’
Theorem

2.20
to the case where we consider the chain complex as a based complex via
\zeta
(
Example

2.15
), and impose the Morse matching in
Examples

2.16
and
2.18
such that the critical sets are
\widetilde{C}_{n}=H_{n}(C)
.
In this case, we verify that
\tau=0
; thus
\widetilde{d}=0
,
h\tau
is trivially nilpotent, and the contraction
(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{h})
is exactly
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
from
Example

2.3
. This is because
\widetilde{f}=f=\alpha
and
\widetilde{g}=g=\beta
are respectively the projection and inclusion maps between
H_{n}(C)
and
C
. To see that
\widetilde{h}_{n}=\gamma_{n}
, we first note that
\widetilde{h}_{n}=h_{n}=-\imath_{n+1}^{(K)}\circ(d_{n+1}^{(BK)})^{-1}\circ\jmath_{n}^{(B)}
, then show that
h_{n}=\gamma_{n}
by expressing
\imath_{n+1}^{(K)},\jmath_{n}^{(B)}
in terms of
\zeta
:

(17)

h_{n}:C_{n}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{n}}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}\twoheadrightarrow B_{n}\xrightarrow[=]{(d_{n+1}^{(BK)})^{-1}}K_{n+1}\hookrightarrow H_{n+1}\oplus B_{n+1}\oplus K_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{n+1}^{-1}}C_{n+1}\xrightarrow{-1}C_{n+1},

where we recall that
d_{n+1}^{(BK)}
recovers the equality
K_{n+1}:=B_{n}
from
Example

2.15
.

In
Section

3.2
, we see how a
P
-graded chain complex equipped with homology decompositions of relative homology groups can be cast as a based complex, and thus allow us to apply
Theorem

2.20
to obtain a contraction. Compared to the case with trivial grading as described in
Example

2.21
, the perturbation
\tau
and differential
\widetilde{d}
of the contracted complex is no longer trivial.

3.
Algebraic Description of the Conley Complex

In this section we prove our main result and provide a derivation of the Conley Complex of a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
. In
Section

3.1
, we achieve this by employing the homological perturbation lemma introduced in
Section

2.1.3
, which extends the contraction of relative homology groups
C^{p}
induced by homology decompositions to a
P
-filtered contraction of the whole complex. In
Section

3.2
, we show that we can equivalently conceptualise the problem of obtaining a Conley Complex as that of finding a Morse matching on
(C,d)
as a based complex, under the framework of
Section

2.3
. We show that homology decompositions of
C^{p}
allow us to express
(C,d)
as a based complex and obtain a Morse matching. Under this choice, we arrive at the same
P
-filtered contraction of
(C,d)
as the one produced with the first approach in
Section

3.1
.

3.1.
The Conley Complex via Contractions of Relative Homology Groups

We first outline a construction that forms the heart of the proof of
Theorem

1.1
. Given a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
, consider the contraction of relative chain complexes
(C^{p},t^{p})
at each poset grade
p\in P
given by elementary contractions induced by a homology decomposition (
Example

2.3
):

(18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H(C^{p}),0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C^{p},t^{p})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{p}}

The contractions above allow us to define a new contraction by taking direct sums over poset grades
p\in P
. On the right hand side of the contraction, the direct sum of
(C^{p},t^{p})
over
p\in P
yields a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,\widehat{d})
; the direct sum yields the same chain groups
C_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}C^{p}_{n}
of the initial complex
(C,d)
by definition, and the differential is given by

(19)

\widehat{d}=\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{p}\circ t^{p}\circ\jmath^{p}

On the left hand side of the contraction, the direct sum yields a
P
-graded chain complex
(\widebar{C},0)
with trivial differentials, where
\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}=H_{n}(C^{p})
and
\widebar{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}
. We let
\bar{\imath}_{n}^{p}:\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}\hookrightarrow\widebar{C}_{n}
and
\bar{\jmath}_{n}^{p}:\widebar{C}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}
denote the natural inclusion and projection onto factors. Note that the chain groups of
\widebar{C}
are isomorphic to those of the Conley complex
\widebar{C}
of
C
, as
\widebar{C}^{p}_{n}\cong H_{n}(C^{p})
for all
p\in P
(
Remark

2.11
). By taking direct sums over
\alpha^{p},\beta^{p},\gamma^{p}
, we obtain a contraction

(20)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the maps are given by

(21)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in P}\bar{\imath}^{p}\circ\alpha^{p}\circ\jmath^{p},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{p}\circ\beta^{p}\circ\bar{\jmath}^{p},

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{p}\circ\gamma^{p}\circ\jmath^{p}.

We note that this is a contraction that only takes the differential within each level set into account.

The main idea of the proof is to perturb
(C,\widehat{d})
by

(22)

\delta\coloneqq d-\widehat{d}=\sum_{p\lneq q}\imath^{p}\circ t^{pq}\circ\jmath^{q}.

Since
\widehat{d}=d-\delta
,
we recover our original complex
(C,d)
by perturbing
(C,\widehat{d})
by
\delta
. In order to apply the perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
) to derive a contraction of
(C,d)
, we first verify the local nilpotency condition.

Lemma 3.1
.

For
\gamma
and
\delta
as defined in
eqs.

21
and
22
respectively,
\gamma\delta
is locally nilpotent for
P
a well-founded poset.

Proof.

To start, notice that
\gamma\delta=\sum_{p<q}\imath^{p}\circ(\gamma^{p}t^{pq})\circ\jmath^{q}
.
Thus, inductively, one can show that

(23)

~(\gamma\delta)^{n}=\underbrace{\gamma\delta\circ\dots\circ\gamma\delta}_{n\text{ times}}=\sum_{p_{n}<\cdots<p_{1}<p_{0}}\imath^{p_{n}}\circ(\gamma^{p_{n}}t^{p_{n}p_{n-1}})\cdots(\gamma^{p_{2}}t^{p_{2}p_{1}})(\gamma^{p_{1}}t^{p_{1}p_{0}})\circ\jmath^{p_{0}}.

Now, suppose that
\gamma\delta
was not locally nilpotent.
Then, there exists some
q\in P
and
v\in C^{q}_{m}
such that
(\gamma\delta)^{n}(v)\neq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consequently, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by
eq.

23
, there exists strictly decreasing chains
p_{n}<\cdots<p_{1}<p_{0}=q
.
Now, since
(\gamma\delta)^{n+1}(v)\neq 0
, there is at least one of these chains that increases by one smallest term, obtaining the chain
p_{n+1}<p_{n}<\cdots<p_{1}<p_{0}=q
. Inductively, we obtain an infinite decreasing chain in
P
, however, this contradicts the hypotheses that
P
is a well-founded poset.
Thus
\gamma\delta
is locally nilpotent.
∎

Consequently,
Lemma

2.4
implies upon perturbing
(C,\widehat{d})
by
\delta
to obtain
(C,d)
, we obtain a contraction

(24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\gamma}}

where
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}
, and

(25)

\displaystyle\widebar{d}

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathcal{S}\beta,

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha+\alpha\mathcal{S}\gamma,

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}

\displaystyle=\beta+\gamma\mathcal{S}\beta,

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\gamma+\gamma\mathcal{S}\gamma.

We now verify that the contraction is
P
-filtered, and the induced differential
\widebar{d}
is strict.

Lemma 3.2
.

The contraction of
(C,d)
obtained by perturbing
(C,\widehat{d})
by
\delta
is
P
-filtered.

Proof.

We first check that
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}
is
P
-filtered by writing it as a sum
\mathcal{S}=\delta\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(\gamma\delta)^{i}
, which is well-defined since we have shown in
Lemma

3.1
that
\gamma\delta
is locally nilpotent. By construction, both
\gamma
and
\delta
are
P
-filtered (see
eqs.

21
and
22
). Since sums, powers and compositions of
P
-filtered maps are
P
-filtered, we thus conclude that
\mathcal{S}
is
P
-filtered.

Since
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
are also
P
-filtered by construction (
eq.

21
),

\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma},\widebar{d}
are all
P
-filtered.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

The differential
\widebar{d}=\alpha\mathcal{S}\beta
obtained by perturbing
(C,\widehat{d})
by
\delta
is strict.

Proof.

Because we have shown above that
\mathcal{S}
is
P
-filtered, and
\alpha,\beta
are
P
-filtered by construction,

\widebar{d}^{p}=\alpha^{p}\mathcal{S}^{p}\beta^{p}
.
By construction
\mathcal{S}^{p}=0
for all
p\in P
since
\delta^{p}=0
.
∎

We have now assembled the relevant material to prove our main theorem.
See
1.1

Proof.

Consider the perturbation
\delta

eq.

22
to the contraction in
eq.

20
:

Since
P
is well-founded, the chain homotopy
\gamma
and perturbation
\delta
are such that
\gamma\delta
are locally nilpotent (
Lemma

3.1
), the conditions of the perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
) are satisfied for the perturbation
\delta
to the contraction in
eq.

20
. By
Lemma

3.2
, the contraction of
(C,d)
onto
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
(
eq.

24
) obtained via the application of the perturbation lemma is
P
-filtered. Thus, the contraction satisfies condition
(M1)
in the definition of the Conley complex (
Definition

2.10
). Furthermore, we have shown that the differential
\widebar{d}
of the contracted complex is strict (
Lemma

3.3
), satisfying condition
(M2)
. Thus,
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
is a Conley complex of
(C,d)
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

We briefly remark on how our approach contrasts with that of
[
36
, Theorem 8.1]
, where they prove the existence of the Conley complex for a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
implicitly constructing a contraction by induction (see also
[
33
, Theorem 5.3.2]
for a contemporary account of the proof). Similar to their approach, our result
Theorem

1.1
also relies on homology decompositions.
Robbin and Salamon
[
36
,
33
]

showed that there exists a
P
-filtered subcomplex
(M,d\rvert_{M})
of
C
which is a Conley complex for
C
.
Our perturbative derivation can be thought of as being ‘dual’ to their approach: our chain groups are the naive choice of relative homology subgroups
\bigoplus_{p\in P}H_{\bullet}(C^{p})
, and we derive a new differential
\widebar{d}
from
(C,d)
using the perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
).

3.2.
The Conley Complex from Morse Matchings

We can also arrive at our main result
Theorem

1.1
for
P
-graded chain complexes via the formalism of algebraic Morse theory for based complexes, as outlined in
Section

2.3
. We do so by expressing
P
-graded complex
(C,d)
equipped with splittings of relative chains as a based complex in the fashion of
Example

3.5
, and defining a Morse matching. This is essentially the same derivation of the Conley complex as that in
Theorem

1.1
, since the theorem that we rely on (
Theorem

2.20
) from
[
39
,
40
]
is itself a consequence of the homological perturbation theory (
Lemma

2.4
) which we apply in
Section

3.1
to arrive at a proof of
Theorem

1.1
. Nonetheless, we present this perspective as it gives a conceptual link to discrete Morse theory, in particular the approach in
[
22
]
where instead of using an algebraic Morse matching, they obtain the connection matrix via applying acyclic partial matchings on the underlying cell complex iteratively.

Following the outline of algebraic Morse theory in
Section

2.3
, we first describe a
P
-graded chain complex, equipped with homology decompositions of relative homology groups
C^{p}
, as a based complex; this builds on our previous example
Example

2.15
for an ‘ungraded’ chain complex equipped with a homology decomposition.

Example 3.5
.

For a
P
-graded chain complex
C
with a homology decomposition
\zeta^{p}_{n}:C^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}
of relative chain groups, we can express
C
as a based complex by writing the chain groups as

C_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}C^{p}_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{p\in P}H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}.

In this case, the index set for each dimension is
I_{n}=P\times\{H_{n},B_{n},K_{n}\}
, if we write
C_{n}^{(p,V)}:=V_{n}^{p}
for
V\in\{H,B,K\}
. We can write the projection and inclusion maps as the composition

\displaystyle\jmath_{n}^{(p,V)}

\displaystyle:C_{n}\overset{\jmath^{p}_{n}}{\twoheadrightarrow}C^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\zeta^{p}_{n}}H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow V^{p}_{n}

\displaystyle\imath_{n}^{(p,V)}

\displaystyle:V^{p}_{n}\hookrightarrow H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow[\cong]{(\zeta^{p}_{n})^{-1}}C^{p}_{n}\xhookrightarrow{\imath^{p}_{n}}C_{n}.

For a pair of poset elements
p\leq q
and
V,W\in\{H,B,K\}
, the homology decomposition of relative chain groups leads to the following restricted differentials:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{p}_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n}^{((p,W)(q,V))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\imath_{n}^{(q,V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\jmath^{(p,W)}_{n-1}}

In particular, consider the case where
p=q
.
Given
V,W\in\{H,B,K\}
, we can write

d_{n}^{((p,W)(p,V))}:V^{p}_{n}\hookrightarrow H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow[\cong]{(\zeta^{p}_{n})^{-1}}C^{p}_{n}\xrightarrow{t^{p}_{n}}C^{p}_{n-1}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\zeta^{p}_{n-1}}H^{p}_{n-1}\oplus B^{p}_{n-1}\oplus K^{p}_{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow W^{p}_{n-1}.

From
Example

2.15
,
we deduce that
d_{n}^{((p,W)(p,V))}\neq 0
only
when
V=K
and
W=B
, in which case
d_{n}^{((p,B)(p,K))}:K_{n}^{p}=B_{n-1}^{p}
is the identity map.

Having formulated a
P
-graded chain complex, equipped with homology decompositions of relative chains, as a based complex, we can now define a Morse matching, generalising the ungraded case in
Example

2.16
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume
P
is a well-founded poset. Given a
P
-graded chain complex of vector spaces
(C,d)
with homology decomposition of relative chains
\zeta^{p}
, consider its expression as a based complex with index set
I_{n}=P\times\{H_{n},B_{n},K_{n}\}
(
Example

3.5
):

C_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{p}H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}.

Then the set of directed edges
\mathsf{M}=\{(p,K_{n})\rightharpoonup(p,B_{n})\ :\ p\in P,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is a Morse matching, and the critical set of
\mathsf{M}
is given in each dimension
n
by
\mathsf{M}^{0}_{n}=\{(p,H_{n})\ :\ p\in P\}
(i.e.
\widetilde{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}H_{n}(C^{p})
).

Proof.

We check that
\mathsf{M}
satisfies the two conditions for a Morse matching as described in
Definition

2.17
.
First, we recall from
Example

3.5
that for all
(p,K_{n})\rightharpoonup(p,B_{n-1})\in\mathsf{M}
there is an isomorphism

d^{((p,B)(p,K))}_{n}=\jmath^{(p,B)}_{n}d_{n}\imath^{(p,K)}_{n}:K_{n}^{p}\xrightarrow{=}B^{p}_{n-1}\ .

Thus the first condition is satisfied.
For the second condition, we need to find a partial order
\preceq_{n}
on
I_{n}
, such that the source and targets of length two directed paths on
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
are ordered by
\preceq_{n}
. We first note that all directed edges in
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
are either of the form
(p,B_{n-1})\rightharpoondown(p,K_{n})
or
(q,V_{n})\rightharpoonup(r,W_{n-1})
for
q\gneq r
. Hence, all length two directed paths on
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
with distinct source and target in
I_{n}
are of either of the following forms, for
p\lneq q
:

\displaystyle(q,V_{n})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup(p,B_{n-1})\rightharpoondown(p,K_{n}),\quad{or}

\displaystyle(q,B_{n})

\displaystyle\rightharpoondown(q,K_{n+1})\rightharpoonup(p,W_{n}).

Consider then the following partial order:
(p,W_{n})\preceq_{n}(q,V_{n})
only if they are identical, or
p\lneq q
.
Since
\leq
is a partial order on
P
, it is straightforward to check that
\preceq_{n}
is a partial order on
I_{n}
, i.e. reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive. We can also verify by inspection that the source and target of any length two path is ordered by
\precneq_{n}
. The partial order
\preceq_{n}
is also well-founded: any descending sequence of unique elements in
I_{n}
must decrease its
P
-grading, and the corresponding descending sequence of unique poset elements must be finite by
P
being well-founded. Having satisfied both conditions in
Definition

2.17
, we have hence shown that
\mathsf{M}
is a Morse matching.

For the final claim, since all the relative pre-boundaries and boundaries are matched by
\mathsf{M}
, what remains are the relative homology groups
H^{p}_{n}
, and thus
\widetilde{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}H^{p}_{n}
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Notice that, if
P
is a finite poset, then
Lemma

2.19
applies and one can alternatively show that
\mathsf{M}
is a Morse matching by the fact that
G(C)^{\mathsf{M}}
is acyclic.

We can now consider the contraction of
(C,d)
induced by the Morse matching in
Lemma

3.6
, according to
Theorem

2.20
. The proposition below states that the Morse matching due to homology decompositions of relative chains induces the same contraction as that in
Theorem

1.1
. Thus this Morse matching yields a compression of a
P
-graded chain complex into its Conley complex, analogous to how acyclic partial matchings on a cell complex reduces the cell complex to an algebraic Morse complex.

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume
P
is a well-founded poset, and
(C,d)
is a
P
-graded chain complex of vector spaces with homology decomposition of relative chains
\zeta^{p}
. Given the Morse matching
\mathsf{M}
on
(C,d)
in
Lemma

3.6
, the corresponding contraction of
(C,d)
as a consequence of
Theorem

2.20
is identical to the
P
-filtered contraction as described in
Theorem

1.1
.

Proof.

We recall that the contraction in
Theorem

2.20
was derived by perturbing the contraction determined by a Morse matching
\mathsf{M}
(
eqs.

13
and
14
)

(
\ast
)

\lxSVG@closescope {{

and the respective perturbations
\tau,\delta
are identical. Then, the contraction of the full complex
(C,d)
obtained by perturbing (

∗

‣
3.2
) by
\tau
is identical to that obtained in
Theorem

1.1
by perturbing (

†

‣
3.2
) by
\delta
.

We have shown in
Lemma

3.6
that
\widetilde{C}=\widebar{C}
as (dimension) graded vector spaces, and thus
(\widetilde{C},0)=(\widebar{C},0)
as chain complexes; all that remains is to show that the perturbations and the maps in the contraction are identical. In other words, that is,
\tau=\delta
, and
(f,g,h)=(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. Let us construct the maps
f,g,h
and
\tau
following the prescription in
eq.

13
. First, the maps
f,g
are simply the projection and inclusion maps
\alpha,\beta
in
eq.

21
. Furthermore, the map
h
in
eq.

13
is equivalent to
\gamma
in
eq.

21
by the observation in
Example

2.21
that in the ungraded case,
\imath^{(K)}_{n+1}\big(d^{(BK)}_{n+1}\big)^{-1}\jmath^{(B)}_{n}=-\gamma_{n}
:

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{(p,K)}_{n+1}\big(d^{((p,B)(p,K))}_{n+1}\big)^{-1}\jmath^{(p,B)}_{n}=\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{p}_{n+1}\gamma^{p}_{n}\jmath^{p}_{n}=\gamma_{n}.

Then, the homology decomposition (
eq.

3
) also implies

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{(b\rightharpoonup a)\notin\mathsf{M}}\imath^{(a)}\circ d^{(ab)}\circ\jmath^{(b)}=d-\sum_{(b\rightharpoonup a)\in\mathsf{M}}\imath^{(a)}\circ d^{(ab)}\circ\jmath^{(b)}=d-\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{(p,B)}d^{((p,B)(p,K))}\jmath^{(p,K)}

\displaystyle=d-\sum_{p\in P}\imath^{p}t^{p}\jmath^{p}=\sum_{p\lneq q}\imath^{p}t^{pq}\jmath^{q}=\delta.

The fourth equality follows from the description of
d^{((p,B)(p,K))}
in
Example

3.5
.
∎

4.
Connection Matrix Algorithm

As we have seen in
Theorem

1.1
choosing homology decompositions for each poset grade
\zeta^{p}_{n}\colon C^{p}_{n}\cong H^{p}_{n}\oplus B^{p}_{n}\oplus K^{p}_{n}
gives rise to a
P
-filtered contraction between
(C,d)
and
(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})
.
A matrix representation of
\widebar{d}
then leads to a connection matrix
\Delta
.
In this section, we develop algorithms to find a suitable splitting and compute connection matrices.
The full connection matrix algorithm is presented in
Algorithm

3
.
The algorithm first involves a routine (
Algorithm

2
) that effects the homology decomposition
\zeta_{p}
of relative chain groups
C^{p}
in a computationally favourable basis, which we call a separating basis (
Definition

4.2
). We give an account of this in
Section

4.1
. We then give a description of the connection matrix
\Delta
for
\widebar{d}
in the separating bases in
Section

4.2
; in particular, we describe the matrix entries
\Delta_{ij}
explicitly in
Corollary

4.9
. Finally, we then describe in
Algorithm

3
how the separating bases can be leveraged to perform a sequence of row and column operations that reduces a boundary matrix representation of
d
in any basis to a connection matrix
\Delta
. We prove in
Proposition

4.11
the correctness of the algorithm:
\Delta
as the output of
Algorithm

3
represents the Conley complex differential
\widebar{d}
in a basis consisting of relative homology cycles at each poset grade.

Henceforth, we assume all chain complexes are finite dimensional (and hence bounded in dimension).

Remark 4.1
.

In the next discussion on matrix algorithms, we make use of the following notation:

•

We use fraktur notation
\mathfrak{A}
to denote an explicit basis of a vector (sub)space. Given a basis
\mathfrak{A}
, we let
\langle\mathfrak{A}\rangle
denote the vector subspace spanned by
\mathfrak{A}
.

•

If
M
is a matrix representing a linear map
f:V\to W
in some explicit bases
\mathfrak{V}
,
\mathfrak{W}
for
V,W
respectively, we write
c(M)
for the set of nonzero columns of
M
; that is, those basis vectors
u\in\mathfrak{V}
such that
f(u)\neq 0
.

•

We implicitly assume a total ordering of basis vectors for any given basis of a vector space, and index all matrices with that total ordering. For any vector
v=\sum_{u\in\mathfrak{A}}v_{u}u
written in a basis
\mathfrak{A}
with coefficients
v_{u}
, we denote the
pivot

\rho(v)
to be the maximal
u\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
v_{u}\neq 0
. Given a subset of vectors
W
, we use the notation
\rho(W)=\{\rho(v)\mid v\in W\}
to denote the associated collection of pivots.

•

For a chain complex with differential
d_{n}
, we use
D_{n}
to denote its matrix representation with respect to a chosen set of bases
\{\mathfrak{A}_{i}\}
of chain groups
C_{i}
.

4.1.
Splittings via the clearing optimisation

Suppose we are given boundary matrices
D_{n}
representing
d_{n}
in bases
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
of
C_{n}
.
In this section, we describe an algorithm that computes split isomorphisms

\zeta_{n}:C_{n}\cong H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}

(
eq.

3
) for all
n
.
In other words, we seek coordinate transformations from an initial basis
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
of
C_{n}
to another basis
\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}_{n}
of
C_{n}
, such that we can write the differential in the new basis as a block matrix
