More formally, we desire a basis
\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}_{n}
for each
C_{n}
, such that:

(1)

\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is a basis of boundary chains:
\mathcal{B}_{n}:=\langle\mathfrak{B}_{n}\rangle=\mathrm{im}(d)=:B_{n-1}
;

(2)

\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is a homology basis:
\ker d=\langle\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}_{n}\rangle
; in other words,
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\langle\mathfrak{H}_{n}\rangle\cong H_{n}
;

(3)

\mathfrak{K}_{n}
is a pre-boundary basis: for
\mathfrak{K}_{n}=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\}
, we have
\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}=\{d(u_{1}),\ldots,d(u_{m})\}
. This implies
d
restricted to
\mathcal{K}_{n}:=\langle\mathfrak{K}_{n}\rangle
is an isomorphism from
\mathcal{K}_{n}
to
\mathcal{B}_{n-1}=B_{n-1}=:K_{n}
.

Next, we present an algorithm that obtains a ‘separating basis’ (see
Definition

4.2
), which enhances the efficiency in the subsequent computations in the main connection matrix algorithm (
Lemmas

4.4
and
4.7
).

Our approach is based on ‘killing reduction’
[
6
]
, which we call
clearing optimisation
following
[
2
]
.
We refer the reader to those references for the correctness of the algorithm, and give a description here.
The algorithm, detailed in
Algorithm

2
, proceeds sequentially starting from the top dimensional boundary matrices. We give an outline of the algorithm:

(1)

At each step, the algorithm performs a column reduction
R_{n}=D_{n}T_{n}
of
D_{n}
by means of the clearing optimisation, assuming that
D_{n+1}
has also been similarly reduced in the previous step, and leveraging data from
R_{n+1}
to avoid unnecessary column operations.

(2)

From the reduction, we then read off the bases
\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n}\subset C_{n}
from columns of
T_{n}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}\subset C_{n-1}
from columns of
R_{n}
.

Since we assume that
C
is bounded, there exists a largest integer
N>0
such that
D_{N}\neq 0
. Because
D_{N+1}=0
, any reduction of
D_{N+1}
must be trivial and
R_{N+1}=0
. If all differentials are trivial, the splitting is immediate.
In each reduction step, the clearing optimisation procedure (
Algorithm

1
) ensures
R_{n}
is column reduced,

T_{n}
is square upper diagonal with ones down the diagonal, and
c(R_{n+1})\subset c(T_{n})
.
In particular, the columns from
T_{n}
are linearly independent and a basis for
C_{n}
.
The desired bases are then obtained by the following partition of column vectors
c(T_{n})=\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}_{n}
:

(26)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{v\in c(T_{n})\mbox{ such that }D_{n}v\neq 0\};

(27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{v\in c(T_{n})\setminus c(R_{n+1})\mbox{ such that }D_{n}v=0\};\quad\text{and}

(28)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{n}

\displaystyle=c(R_{n+1}).

Note that
d(\mathfrak{K}_{n})=c(R_{n})=\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}
, and thus
d
restricted to
\langle\mathfrak{K}_{n}\rangle
is an isomorphism onto
\langle c(R_{n})\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}\rangle
; the non-zero columns of
R_{n+1}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is a basis for
B_{n}
; and
\ker d=\langle\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}_{n}\rangle
. It follows from the properties above that this choice of basis gives an explicit matrix representation of the splitting isomorphisms
eq.

3
:

(29)

\zeta_{n}:C_{n}=\langle c(T_{n})\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{H}_{n}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathfrak{B}_{n}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathfrak{K}_{n}\rangle=\mathcal{H}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{B}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{n}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}\oplus B_{n}\oplus K_{n}.

Algorithm

1
is presented for a matrix
D
and a set
S
, which, in our present case, would correspond to the pair
(D_{n},c(R_{n+1}))
.
The clearing optimisation reduces column operations by ‘pre-computing’ the columns of
T_{n}
with information from
R_{n+1}
.
Basically, each column
b\in c(R_{n+1})
gives the
\rho(b)
column in
T_{n}
. In addition, for each
b\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, since
D_{n}b=0
, one can set the
\rho(b)
-column from
D_{n}
to zero, before proceeding with the usual column reduction. Thus, this procedure enforces
c(R_{n+1})\subset c(T_{n})
.

Algorithm 1

clear_reduce

Data:

(1)

Matrix
D
of size
M\times N
; and

(2)

a set
S
of linearly independent vectors such that
\langle S\rangle\subset\ker(D)
.

Result:

Matrices
R,T
with
R=DT
,
R
column reduced,
T
upper-triangular and
S\subset c(T)
.

1

T\leftarrow\mathrm{id}_{N}
;

2

R\leftarrow D
;

3

for

b\in S

do

4

Set
\rho(b)
-column from
T
equal to
b
;

5

Set
\rho(b)
-column from
R
equal to
0
;

6

end for

7

for

i\leftarrow M

down to

1

do

8

Q\leftarrow\{1\leq j\leq N\mid\rho(R[j])=i\}
;

9

if

Q\neq\emptyset

then

10

m\leftarrow\min(Q)
;

11

for

j\in Q\setminus\{m\}

do

12

a\leftarrow R[i,j]/R[i,m]
;

13

R[:,j]\leftarrow R[:,j]-a\cdot R_{n}[:,m]
;

14

T[:,j]\leftarrow T[:,j]-a\cdot T[:,m]
;

15

end for

16

end if

17

end for

18

return

R,T

Algorithm 2

separating_via_clearing

Data:

Sequence of matrices
(D_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
with
D_{i}D_{i+1}=0
for all
0\leq i<N
and
D_{0}=0
.

Result:

Separating bases
((\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N}
, matrices
(T_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
returned by
clear_reduce

1

\mathfrak{B}_{N}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

2

for

n\leftarrow N

down to

0

do

3

R_{n},T_{n}\leftarrow\texttt{clear\_reduce}(D_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n})
;

4

\mathfrak{H}_{n}\leftarrow\{v\in c(T_{n})\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mbox{ such that }D_{n}v=0\}
;

5

if

n>0

then

6

\mathfrak{K}_{n}\leftarrow\{v\in c(T_{n})\mbox{ such that }D_{n}v\neq 0\}
;

7

\mathfrak{B}_{n-1}\leftarrow c(R_{n})
;

8

end if

9

end for

10

\mathfrak{K}_{0}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

11

return

((\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N}
,
(T_{n})_{n=0}^{N}

Furthermore, in
Lemma

4.3
, we see that
(\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n})
is a separating basis for
C_{n}
in the sense of the following definition:

Definition 4.2
.

The triple
(\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n})
forms a
separating basis
for
C_{n}
(with respect to
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
) if the following conditions are satisfied:

(S1)

for all elements
z\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
and all
w\in\mathfrak{K}_{n}
it follows that
z_{\beta}=w_{\beta}=0
for all
\beta\in\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
,

(S2)

for fixed
z\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
, it follows that, for all
v\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}\setminus\{z\}
and all
w\in\mathfrak{K}_{n}
,

v_{\rho(z)}=w_{\rho(z)}=0
, and

(S3)

for all
z\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
we have
Z_{\rho(z)}=1_{k}
.

Altogether, given a sequence of differentials
(D_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
, one obtains, by the aforementioned procedure, a sequence of separating bases
((\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N}
; this is outlined in
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

(\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n})
is a separating basis for
C_{n}
(w.r.t.
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
).

Proof.

Recall the description of the clearing optimisation outlined in
Algorithm

1
.
In particular, recall that all columns from
D_{n}
indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
are set to zero before proceeding with the column reductions.
One can then assume that
Item

(S1)
is satisfied, since performing column operations in
D_{n}
, all columns from
\rho(\mathfrak{B}_{n})
were zero.
We can even refine further.
Indeed, since each
z\in\mathfrak{H}_{n}
is such that
D_{n}z=0
, then, if we followed the reduction of
D_{n}
using
Algorithm

1
, one never adds the column labelled by
\rho(z)
from
D_{n}
to columns on its right, since it becomes trivial.
Thus,
Item

(S2)
follows.
In addition, when performing left-to right column additions in
Algorithm

1
, the target column is not multiplied by any scalar, and so
Item

(S3)
automatically holds.
∎

For convenience, for any
V\in\{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{K}\}
we write
V\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{n}
.
Similarly, given
\mathfrak{D}\in\{\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{K}\}
, we write
\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{D}_{n}
and
\zeta(\mathfrak{D})=\{\zeta(z)\colon z\in\mathfrak{D}\}
.
By construction,
Algorithm

2
leads to separating bases
((\mathfrak{H}_{n},\mathfrak{B}_{n},\mathfrak{K}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N}
for
C_{n}
(w.r.t.
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
) for all
0\leq n\leq N+1
.
Choosing trivial bases for other degrees, we obtain a separating basis
(\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{K})
for
C
(w.r.t.
\mathfrak{A}
).

Separating bases have properties that render them computationally convenient:
given a subset of basis vectors
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{A}
, we define the projection
\jmath_{\mathfrak{D}}\colon C\rightarrow\langle\mathfrak{D}\rangle
which is given by sending

v=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}}v_{\alpha}\alpha

to

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{D}}v_{\alpha}\alpha,

for all
v\in C
.
In addition, we also consider projections (not necessarily chain maps)
\jmath_{V}\colon C\rightarrow V
for
V\in\{H,B,K\}
, derived from the isomorphisms
\zeta\colon C\cong H\oplus B\oplus K
(
eq.

29
).
Lastly, we introduce the map
\theta\colon\langle\rho(\mathfrak{H})\rangle\rightarrow H
given by
\theta(\rho(z))=\zeta(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{H}
; this is an isomorphism since the pivots from
\mathfrak{H}
are all distinct.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{K})
be a separating basis for
C
.
Then,

(i)

\ker(\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{B})})=\ker(\jmath_{B})=\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle
, and

(ii)

(\jmath_{H})_{\mid\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}}=(\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})})_{\mid\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}}
.

Proof.

First, suppose that
v\in C
is such that
\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{B})}(v)=0
; in particular,
v\in\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle
.
On the other hand,
Item

(S1)
implies that
\langle\mathfrak{H}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}\rangle\subset\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle
.
Which, together with

\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})=\rho\big(\mbox{$\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c(T_{n})\big)\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})$}=\mbox{$\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho(c(T_{n})\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{n})$}=\mbox{$\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho(\mathfrak{H}_{n}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}_{n})$}=\rho(\mathfrak{H}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}),

implies that
\langle\mathfrak{H}\sqcup\mathfrak{K}\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle
. Hence
v\in\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
and so, by
eq.

29
, it follows that
\jmath_{B}(v)=0
.
Now, suppose that
v\in C
is such that
\jmath_{B}(v)=0
.
Then
v\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}=\langle\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})\rangle
, and it follows that
\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{B})}(v)=0
, so that
Item

(i)
holds.

Let us now show
Item

(ii)
.
For this, recall that, given
v\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
, one can write

v=\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}z+\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{K}}v_{w}w
for unique coefficients
v_{z},v_{w}\in k
for all
z\in\mathfrak{H}
and
w\in\mathfrak{K}
.
In particular, it follows that

\jmath_{H}(v)=\jmath_{H}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}z+\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{K}}v_{w}w\right)=\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}\zeta(z),

where we have used
\jmath_{H}(z)=\zeta(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{H}
and
\jmath_{H}(w)=0
for all
w\in\mathfrak{K}
.
Similarly, one obtains

\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(v)=\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}z+\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{K}}v_{w}w\right)=\theta\left(\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}\rho(z)\right)=\sum_{z\in\mathfrak{H}}v_{z}\zeta(z).

where we have used
\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(z)=\rho(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{H}
and
\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(w)=0
for all
w\in\mathfrak{K}
, which, in turn, follow from
Item

(S2)
and
Item

(S3)
of separating bases.
Thus,
Item

(ii)
holds.
∎

4.2.
The Conley differential and the Connection matrix under a Separating Bases

In this subsection, we deduce a technical result concerning the Conley Complex for a given choice of separating bases for
C^{p}
for all
p\in P
.
Namely, in
Proposition

4.7
we provide a formula for the Conley differential.
This allows to explicitly express the entries of the Connection matrix for a particular choice of separating bases; see
Corollary

4.9
.

To start, we consider separating bases taken at the relative level; i.e. there exist separating bases
(\mathfrak{H}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{B}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{K}^{p}_{n})
such that there are relative chain decompositions
C^{p}_{n}=\mathcal{H}^{p}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{B}^{p}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{p}_{n}
with
\mathcal{H}^{p}_{n}=\langle\mathfrak{H}^{p}_{n}\rangle
,
\mathcal{B}^{p}_{n}=\langle\mathfrak{B}^{p}_{n}\rangle
and
\mathcal{K}^{p}_{n}=\langle\mathfrak{K}^{p}_{n}\rangle
for all
p\in P
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, as in
eq.

29
.

Remark 4.5
.

For convenience, for any
V\in\{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{K}\}
we write
V\coloneqq\bigoplus_{p\in P,n\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{p}_{n}
.
Similarly, given
\mathfrak{D}\in\{\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{K}\}
, we write
\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{p\in P,n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{D}_{n}^{p}
and

\zeta(\mathfrak{D})=\bigsqcup_{p\in P,n\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta^{p}_{n}(\mathfrak{D}_{n}^{p})
.
Altogether, we have the following decomposition for
C
:

C=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{K}=\langle\mathfrak{H}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathfrak{B}\rangle\oplus\langle\mathfrak{K}\rangle\cong H\oplus B\oplus K\ ,

and we say that
(\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{K})
is a separating basis for
C
(w.r.t.
\mathfrak{A}
).

Lemma 4.6
.

\mathrm{im}(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)=\ker(\gamma)=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

First, we have
\mathrm{im}(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)\subset\ker(\gamma)

by the equality
\gamma+\gamma\widehat{d}\gamma=0
which holds by applying the properties of chain contractions from
eq.

7
to the contraction in
eq.

20
.
Next, the other inclusion
\ker(\gamma)\subset\mathrm{im}(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)
follows by
(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)(\ker(\gamma))=\ker(\gamma)
.
Hence,
\mathrm{im}(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)=\ker(\gamma)
holds.

On the other hand,
by the definition of
\gamma
from
eq.

7
,
\gamma
restricts to an isomorphism

\gamma_{|\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\cong B\xrightarrow{=}K\cong\mathcal{K}
.
Hence, it follows that
\mathcal{K}=\mathrm{im}(\gamma)
and
\ker(\gamma)\subset\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
.
In addition,
the equality
\gamma^{2}=0
implies that
\mathcal{K}=\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\ker(\gamma)
. Also,
\mathcal{H}=\mathrm{im}(\beta)\subset\ker(\gamma)
by
\gamma\beta=0
. Hence,
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}\subset\ker(\gamma)
.
∎

Next, we recall the contraction in
eq.

24
which is given by

(30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widebar{C},\widebar{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\gamma}}

where

\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}
and
\widebar{d}=\alpha\mathcal{S}\beta
.
Recall that
\widebar{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}H^{p}_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
.
In this context
\alpha=\bigoplus_{p\in P}\jmath_{H^{p}}
and
\beta=\bigoplus_{p\in P}\imath_{H^{p}}
.
Next, we introduce a useful description of the differential
\widebar{d}
of the Conley complex that holds when choosing separating bases.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H}^{p},\mathfrak{B}^{p},\mathfrak{K}^{p})
are be separating bases for
C^{p}
for all
p\in P
.
Suppose that we have a contraction as in
eq.

30
.
Then,
\widebar{d}(z)=\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta(z)
for all
z\in\widebar{C}
.

Proof.

To start, since
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}
, we obtain

\mathcal{S}-\delta\gamma\mathcal{S}=\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}-\delta\gamma\delta(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}=\delta(1-\delta\gamma)(1-\gamma\delta)^{-1}=\delta

In particular,
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})
.

Next, since
\alpha
is a chain map then
\alpha\widehat{d}=0\alpha=0
, and it follows that
\alpha d=\alpha(\widehat{d}+\delta)=\alpha\delta
.
Hence,

(31)

\widebar{d}=\alpha\mathcal{S}\beta=\alpha\delta(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta=\alpha d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta\ .

On the other hand, we claim that
\mathrm{im}(d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta)\subseteq\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
.
For this, recalling that
\widehat{d}\beta=\beta 0=0
, we obtain

d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta=(\delta+\widehat{d})(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta=\delta(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta+\widehat{d}\gamma\mathcal{S}\beta=\mathcal{S}\beta+\widehat{d}\gamma\mathcal{S}\beta=(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)\mathcal{S}\beta\ ,

which implies the claim by
Lemma

4.6
, since
\mathrm{im}(1+\widehat{d}\gamma)=\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
.

To finish, given
z\in\widebar{C}
, we write
v=d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta(z)
and, using
Item

(ii)
from
Lemma

4.4
, it follows that
\jmath_{H^{p}}(v^{p})=\theta^{p}\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H}^{p})}(v^{p})
for all
p\in P
.
Altogether, noticing that

\alpha=\jmath_{H}
and using
eq.

31
, we obtain

\widebar{d}(z)=\alpha(v)=\jmath_{H}(v)=\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(v)

Thus, restricting the column
v
to the entries indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{H})
leads to the coordinates of
\widebar{d}(z)
in terms of the image
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
; which is a basis for
\widebar{C}
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

A consequence of
Proposition

4.7
is that given
z\in H
, the image under the Conley differential
\widebar{d}(z)
corresponds to a chain
d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta(z)\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
.
This construction adapts work from Section 3 in
[
21
]
to the context of
P
-graded complexes. In Definition 3.8. from
[
21
]
pre-boundaries are called
\mathcal{K}
-chains
and elements from
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
are called
canonical chains
.

We wish to compute the connection matrix
\Delta
in the fixed basis
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
for the
P
-graded space
H
.
To do this, for each
z\in\mathfrak{H}
, we need to compute the coefficients of
\widebar{d}(z)
in terms of
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
.
The following result summarises this expression. It is an immediate consequence from
Proposition

4.7
.

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H}^{p},\mathfrak{B}^{p},\mathfrak{K}^{p})
be a separating basis for
C^{p}
for all
p\in P
.
Denote by
\Delta
the connection matrix; the matrix associated to
\widebar{d}
with respect to the basis
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
.
Given two generators
z_{i},z_{j}\in\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
, denote by
\Delta_{i,j}
the entry of
\Delta
from the row
i
and the column
j
(i.e. associated to
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
respectively). Then,

\Delta_{i,j}=\theta\jmath_{\rho(z_{i})}d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta(z_{j})\ .

4.3.
Connection matrix algorithm

In this subsection, we present a simple procedure to obtain a connection matrix.
Recall that
C_{n}=\bigoplus_{p\in P}C^{p}_{n}
and suppose that we have fixed bases
\mathfrak{A}^{p}_{n}
for each term
C^{p}_{n}
.
Putting together these bases we obtain a basis
\mathfrak{A}
for
C
.
Now, let
D
be the boundary matrix of
C
in the basis
\mathfrak{A}
, assuming that columns and rows are sorted in a compatible way with the poset
P
and dimensions.
That is, we impose a total order in
\mathfrak{A}
so that, given two generators
\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}^{p}_{n}
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{A}^{q}_{m}
with either
p<q
or (
p=q
and
n<m
), then
\alpha<\beta
.

Given
p\in P
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
D[p,p,n]
the restriction of the matrix
D
to the columns indexed by
\mathfrak{A}^{p}_{n}
and the rows indexed by
\mathfrak{A}^{p}_{n-1}
.
On the other hand, we denote by
D[:,p]
the restriction of
D
to the columns indexed by
\mathfrak{A}^{p}
and the rows indexed by
\mathfrak{A}
.
As mentioned above,
D
denotes the boundary matrix of
C
in the basis
\mathfrak{A}
.
In this case
D[p,p,n]
is the matrix associated to the twisted differential
t^{p}_{n}\colon C^{p}_{n}\rightarrow C^{p}_{n-1}
.
while
D[:,p]
is the matrix associated to the composition
d\circ\imath^{p}\colon C^{p}\rightarrow C
.

We perform transformations on
D
to obtain a connection matrix
\Delta
.
In order to describe these operations, we refer to columns and rows being labelled by elements from
\mathfrak{A}
, which are the generators of the bases associated to the initial matrix
D
.
In particular, one needs to follow four steps:

(I)

Using the clearing optimisation, reduce the matrices
D[p,p,n]
; this is done by elementary column operations on
D
.
Notice that this step can be executed in parallel over
p\in P
. This leads to separating bases
(\mathfrak{H}_{n}^{p},\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{p},\mathfrak{K}_{n}^{p})
for
C_{n}^{p}
, all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p\in P
.

(II)

(Optional) Remove from
D
all rows indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{K})
and all columns indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
.

(III)

For each
w\in\mathfrak{K}^{p}_{n}
, add the column from
D
indexed by
\rho(w)
to other columns sharing the same pivot
\rho(d(w))
, so that the row labelled by the pivot
\rho(d(w))
has a single non-zero entry (the one for the column indexed by
\rho(w)
).
Here, notice that the column indexed by
\rho(w)
will only be added to columns indexed by generators in
\mathfrak{A}^{q}
with
q>p
(since, at the start of the procedure,
D
is the matrix of
d
, a
P
-filtered function).
This operation can be performed following any order, and applying the column operations on
D
.

(IV)

Remove all remaining rows and columns indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{K})
or
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
.

For the reader who wants more details about this procedure, we provide a pseudocode instantiation next.
In our description, the execution of the optional
Item

(II)
is mixed with
Item

(I)
and we do not discuss parallelisation. The method is summarised in
Algorithm

3
:

•

[lines 1-9]

Item

(I)
and
Item

(II)
are executed within the first for loop.
For each
p\in P
, we start by computing the separating bases
((\mathfrak{H}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{B}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{K}^{p}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N}
as well as the diagonal matrices
(T_{n})_{n=0}^{N}
by calling
Algorithm

2
. After, we consider a matrix
T=\textrm{Diag}((T_{n})_{n=0}^{N})
which is the diagonal matrix resulting from putting the matrices
T_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq N
along the diagonal blocks.
Then,
Item

(II)
allows removing a few rows and columns from
D
and
T
.
We end each for loop by replicating the column operations that lead to the separating bases on the column block
D[:,p]
by simply computing the matrix product
D[:,p]T
.

•

[lines 10-19]

Item

(III)
is executed within the second for loop. Given
w\in\mathfrak{K}
and the pivot
\alpha=\rho(w)
, we denote by
\tau
the pivot from the column
D[:,\alpha]
.
Then, this column is added to other columns
D[:,\beta]
indexed by
\beta\in\mathfrak{A}_{\dim(\alpha)}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{B})
(as we removed the columns
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
in
Item

(II)
) such that their pivot is also
\tau
.
Since
D
is the matrix of a
P
-filtered differential, this implies that
\alpha<\beta
.

•

[lines 20-21]
Finally,
Item

(IV)
is executed.

Algorithm 3

Connection matrix computation (3 steps)

Data:

The matrix
D
of
P
-filtered differential
d\colon C\rightarrow C
with respect to a fixed basis
\mathfrak{A}
(with an order compatible with
P
).

Result:

The connection matrix
\Delta

1

for

p\in P

do

2

((\mathfrak{H}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{B}^{p}_{n},\mathfrak{K}^{p}_{n}))_{n=0}^{N},(T_{n})_{n=0}^{N}\leftarrow\texttt{separating\_via\_clearing}((D[p,p,n])_{n=0}^{N})
;

3

T\leftarrow\textrm{Diag}((T_{n})_{n=0}^{N})
;

4

D\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_rows}(\rho(\mathfrak{K}^{p}),D)
;

5

D\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_columns}(\rho(\mathfrak{B}^{p}),D)
;

6

T\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_rows}(\rho(\mathfrak{B}^{p}),T)
;

7

T\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_columns}(\rho(\mathfrak{B}^{p}),T)
;

8

D[:,p]\leftarrow D[:,p]T
;

9

end for

10

for

w\in\mathfrak{K}

do

11

\alpha\leftarrow\rho(w)
;

12

\tau\leftarrow\rho(D[:,\alpha])
;

13

for

\beta\in\mathfrak{A}_{\dim(\alpha)}\setminus\rho(\mathcal{B})

do

14

if

\rho(D[:,\beta])=\tau

then

15

a\leftarrow D[\tau,\beta]/D[\tau,\alpha]

;
//
Conditional execution implies
\alpha<\beta

16

D[:,\beta]\leftarrow a\cdot D[:,\alpha]
;

17

end if

18

end for

19

end for

20

D\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_columns}(\rho(\mathfrak{K}),D)
;

21

D\leftarrow\texttt{remove\_rows}(\rho(\mathfrak{B}),D)
;

22

return

D

After executing this procedure, the result is a matrix
\Delta
which is associated to a map
\widebar{C}\rightarrow\widebar{C}[-1]
with respect to a fixed basis
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})

Correctness of this procedure will follow by showing that the entries from
\Delta
correspond to the description from
Corollary

4.9
.
However, let us first consider an example.

Example 4.10
.

In this example, we compute a connection matrix for the complex
X
from
Example

2.12
. To start, we perform
Item

(I)
on the differential matrix
D
associated to
d\colon C(X)\rightarrow C(X)[-1]
depicted on the left below, where one needs to reduce the relative boundaries, marked by blocks.
The result is the matrix on the right; the only operation performed consists in adding the column labelled by
\overline{vu}
to the column labelled by
\overline{vw}
.
As a result, the column labelled by
\overline{vw}
stores the boundary of a sum
d(\overline{vu}+\overline{vw})
.
Next, we remove the rows indexed by elements from
\mathfrak{K}
and the columns indexed by elements from
\mathfrak{B}
(we shade these):

\left(\begin{array}[]{c|ccccccc}&u&w&\overline{uw}&v&\overline{vu}&\overline{vw}&\overline{uvw}\\
\hline\cr u&\hbox to5pt{\vbox to6.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.22221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Additionally, we obtain the following decomposition of relative chains:

\displaystyle C^{0}=

\displaystyle\langle u\rangle=\mathcal{H}^{0}_{0},

\displaystyle C^{1}=

\displaystyle\langle w\rangle\oplus\langle\overline{uw}\rangle=\mathcal{B}^{1}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{1}_{1},

\displaystyle C^{2}=

\displaystyle\langle v\rangle\oplus\langle\overline{vu}\rangle\oplus\langle\overline{vu}+\overline{vw}\rangle=\mathcal{B}^{2}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{2}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle C^{3}=

\displaystyle\langle\overline{uvw}\rangle=\mathcal{H}^{3}_{2}

On the left in the picture below, we show the result after performing
Item

(III)
, which, in this case, consists in adding the column labelled by
\overline{uw}
to the column labelled by
\overline{vw}
.
In this case, the shaded rows must have only a single non-zero entry.
On the right, we show the result after performing
Item

(IV)
, removing the shaded rows and columns; this leads to a connection matrix:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c>{\columncolor{gray!20}}c>{\columncolor{gray!20}}ccccc}&u&\overline{uw}&\overline{vu}&\overline{vw}&\overline{uvw}\\
\hline\cr u&0&1&1&0&0\\
w&0&1&&0&0\\
v&0&&1&0&0\\
\overline{vw}&0&&&0&1\\
\overline{uvw}&0&&&0&0\end{array}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)=\Delta.

Also, since
\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{0}\sqcup\mathfrak{H}^{2}_{1}\sqcup\mathfrak{H}^{3}_{2}
with
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{0}=\{u\}
,
\mathfrak{H}^{2}_{1}=\{\overline{vu}+\overline{vw}\}
and
\mathfrak{H}^{3}_{2}=\{\overline{uvw}\}
, we have that
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})=\{u+B_{0}^{0},\overline{vu}+\overline{vw},\overline{uvw}\}
is a basis for
\widebar{C}=H^{0}_{0}\oplus H^{2}_{1}\oplus H^{3}_{2}
. Altogether, recall that, the connection matrix
\Delta
is the matrix associated to the differential
\widebar{d}\colon\widebar{C}\rightarrow\widebar{C}_{-1}
with respect to this basis
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
.

Proposition 4.11
.

The output

\Delta
of
Algorithm

3
is the connection matrix
representing
\widebar{d}
with respect to the basis
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
, which consists of cycle bases of relative homology groups
H^{p}_{n}
.

Proof.

To start, denote by
\widetilde{D}
the resulting matrix after performing
Item

(I)
and
Item

(III)
, without having executed
Item

(II)
, from the procedure.
Given
z\in\mathfrak{H}^{p}_{n}
, consider the column from
\widetilde{D}
indexed by
\rho(z)
, which is a chain
v\in C_{n-1}
.
Notice that, by construction, it follows that

v=d\bigg(z+\sum_{q<p}\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{K}^{q}_{n}}wc_{w}\bigg)

for suitable coefficients
c_{w}\in k
for all
w\in\mathfrak{K}^{q}_{n}
and all
q<p
. Indeed, only columns indexed by
\rho(w)
with
w\in\mathfrak{K}^{q}_{n}
can have been added to the column indexed by
\rho(z)
, and, since
\rho(d(w))\in\mathfrak{A}^{q}\subset C^{q}
, it must be that
q<p
.

Next,
v\in C_{n-1}=\bigoplus_{q\in P}C^{q}_{n-1}
can be written as
v=\sum_{q\in P}v^{q}
for unique chains
v^{p}\in C^{p}_{n-1}
for all
p\in P
.
In addition, by the description of
Item

(III)
from the algorithm, it follows that
\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{B})}(v)=0
.
Now, since, by
Item

(I)
,
(\mathfrak{H}^{q}_{n-1},\mathfrak{B}^{q}_{n-1},\mathfrak{K}^{q}_{n-1})
is a separating basis, using
Item

(i)
from
Lemma

4.4
, it follows that
\jmath_{B}(v)=0
for all
q\in P
.
This implies that
v\in\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
and, by
Lemma

4.6
, also
\gamma(v)=0
.
Another consequence, by
Lemma

4.4

Item

(ii)
is that
\jmath_{H}(v)=\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(v)
and all coordinates from
v
which are in
\mathfrak{A}\setminus\rho(\mathfrak{H})
can be ignored.
Hence,
Item

(II)
can be executed without affecting the end result since the column operations performed during
Item

(III)
remain the same.
Notice that the columns optionally removed in
Item

(II)
are indexed by
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
, so these are never added to other columns.
Thus, removing them has no effect on the final result, after executing
Item

(III)
and
Item

(IV)
.
On the other hand, the rows optionally removed in
Item

(II)
are
\rho(\mathfrak{K})
and do not contain relative pivots (i.e.
\rho(\mathfrak{B})
), so their removal also has no effect on the final result.
These optional removals reduce the unnecessary operations when executing
Item

(III)
.

Next, let us write
y=\sum_{q<p}\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{K}^{q}_{n}}wc_{w}
.
We claim that
y=\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)
.
If this claim is true, then we have shown the correctness of the algorithm, as
v
is a chain representing
d(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})\beta(z)
and
\zeta(z)\in\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
. The last
Item

(IV)
returns the coordinates of
\theta\jmath_{\rho(\mathfrak{H})}(v)
with respect to
\zeta(\mathfrak{H})
; these are the coordinates of the connection matrix by
Corollary

4.9
.

Let us now prove the claim.
In particular, we will show the equivalent statement that
\jmath^{s}(y)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)
for all
s\in P
, where
\jmath^{s}\colon C\twoheadrightarrow C^{s}
denotes the projection map.
To start,
\jmath^{s}(y)=0
for all
s\geq p
and in this case the claim trivially holds since
\jmath^{s}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)=0
.
Let us now consider
s\in P
such that
s<p
and there does not exist
r\in P
such that
s<r<p
.
In this case, using that
t^{sq}\neq 0
implies
s\leq q
, it follows that

\jmath^{s}(v)=\jmath^{s}d(z+y)=t^{sp}(z)+\sum_{q<p}t^{sq}\jmath^{q}(y)=t^{sp}(z)+t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)\ .

Now, since
\gamma\jmath^{s}(v)=\jmath^{s}\gamma(v)=0
, and since
y\in\mathcal{K}
, we deduce that

\jmath^{s}(y)=-\gamma t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)=\gamma t^{sp}(z)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\delta(z).

Furthermore, using again our hypotheses on
s
, it follows that
\jmath^{s}(\gamma\delta)^{m}(z)=0
for all
m>1
, and so

\jmath^{s}(y)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\delta(z)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)
.
We now proceed inductively.

By induction, let
s<p
and suppose that, for all
q\in P
such that
s<q<p
, the equality
\jmath^{q}(y)=\jmath^{q}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)
holds.
In such case

(32)

\jmath^{s}(v)=\jmath^{s}d(z+y)=t^{sp}(z)+t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)+\sum_{s<q<p}t^{sq}\jmath^{q}(y)\\
=t^{sp}(z)+t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)+\sum_{s<q<p}t^{sq}\jmath^{q}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)=t^{sp}(z)+t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)+\jmath^{s}\delta\gamma\mathcal{S}(z),

where, for the last equality, we have used that
\jmath^{s}\delta=\sum_{s<q}t^{sq}\jmath^{q}
together with the fact that
\jmath^{\ell}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)=0
for all
\ell\geq p
.
On the other hand, in order, using
\gamma\jmath^{s}(v)=0
together with
eq.

32
,
t^{sp}(z)=\jmath^{s}\delta(z)
,
\gamma\jmath^{s}=\jmath^{s}\gamma
, and
\mathcal{S}=\delta(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})
, it follows

\jmath^{s}(y)=-\gamma t^{s}\jmath^{s}(y)=\gamma t^{sp}(z)+\gamma\jmath^{s}\delta\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)=\gamma\jmath^{s}\delta(z)+\gamma\jmath^{s}\delta\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\delta(1+\gamma\mathcal{S})(z)=\jmath^{s}\gamma\mathcal{S}(z).

This finishes the inductive proof (since
P
is well-founded) of the claim
y=\gamma\mathcal{S}(z)
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

The method presented in this section is very similar to the procedure to obtain the connection matrix from
[
11
,
9
]
.
In particular, after performing
Item

(I)
, relative boundaries are the same as
homogeneous columns
.
However, our method differs from those references, since it first computes relative homology groups via the clearing optimisation in
Item

(I)
. In contrast, the algorithm in
[
11
,
9
]
proceeds in a single for-loop ranging over the columns of the matrix
D
.
Our preprocessing of relative homology can be parallelized and provides further computational advantages.
Namely, one can execute
Item

(II)
, which deletes rows and columns from
D
, before executing
Item

(III)
, which adds columns indexed by generators at different poset grades.

Algorithmic complexity

The method presented in this section has an algorithmic complexity bounded above by
N^{3}
, where
N=\#\mathfrak{A}
, obtaining a similar bound to the computation of ordinary persistent homology as done in
[
11
,
9
]
.
We give a derivation below.
Recall that
\mathfrak{A}^{p}
denote the bases for
C^{p}
for all
p\in P
, which compose a basis
\mathfrak{A}
for
C
.
Let
M=\max\{\#\mathfrak{A}^{p}\}
.

Item

(I)
takes a complexity of
NM^{2}
times the size of the poset
\#P
; computing separating bases has a complexity of about
M^{3}
time and replicating the same reductions on the block
D[:,p]
is then bounded by
NM^{2}
.
On the other hand, let
K=\#\mathfrak{B}=\#\mathfrak{K}
be the total number of columns being relative boundaries (or preboundaries) only.
Assuming that the optional
Item

(II)
has been executed,
Item

(III)
takes about
K(N-K)^{2}
time, since the
K
relative preboundary columns might be added to the
N-K
columns that are not boundaries, whose length is also
N-K
.
Notice that executing
Item

(II)
and
Item

(IV)
has a negligible cost, compared to the previous two.
Thus, the total complexity is of
NM^{2}(\#P)+K(N-K)^{2}
. In this case,
K
is only known after executing
Item

(I)
, while the other two
M
and
N
are known from the start.
As mentioned earlier, one could parallelize the computation of
Item

(I)
, which would divide
\#P
by the number of available processors.

5.
Conclusion and Future Work

Our definition of the connection matrix is based on the notion of Conley complexes from
[
22
, Def. 4.23]
. We have shown how to combine algebraic Morse theory, in particular algebraic Morse matchings (see
Definition

2.17
), with a homological perturbation lemma (
Lemma

2.4
) to obtain the chain contraction of the Conley complex (
Theorem

1.1
).
The upshot of this analysis is an explicit description of the Conley complex differential, stated in
Proposition

4.7
, and of the coefficients in the connection matrix, stated in
Corollary

4.9
.
In
Section

4.1
, we then employed the clearing optimisations from
[
6
,
2
]
to find a splitting of the chain group
C_{n}
into a sum of Morse chains, boundaries and pre-boundaries. Conveniently, this procedure leads to a separating basis for the chain group
C_{n}
from a Gaussian reduction of the matrix describing the chain complex differential.
By adding suitable columns labeled by pre-boundaries to the columns of
D
we then modified
D
to obtain the connection matrix by following the algorithm outlined in
Section

4.3
.

The description of the connection matrix in terms of the infinite sum is intriguing: each summand adds homological information from deeper and deeper layers of the poset. A similar approximation of homology can be found in spectral sequences and the spectral systems from
[
30
]
.
By regarding a
P
-graded chain complex
(C,d)
as an
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered chain complex (see
Remark

2.6
),
[
41
]
showed that one can obtain a spectral system via its Cartan-Eilenberg system, involving relative homology groups of
\mathsf{O}(P)
-filtered subcomplexes of
(C,d)
.
Furthermore, by Theorem 5.1. from
[
41
]
such Cartain-Eilenberg systems are invariant under
O(P)
-filtered chain isomorphisms, so a question would be if these systems can be explicitly computed using connection matrices.
As future work, we intend to investigate the computational relationships between the homological perturbation theory presented here and the successive derivations in the spectral systems of the Cartan-Eilenberg system of
(C,d)
.

Our algorithm relies on homology decompositions, which is a convenience due to only considering chain complexes over vector spaces with field coefficients. Algebraic Morse theory, however, works with coefficients in arbitrary
R
-modules for a commutative ring
R
. Therefore another interesting direction of research would be to adapt the description of the connection matrix and ultimately also the algorithm to this setting.

Since the connection matrix is dependent on a choice of basis, we can also consider how that choice can be made in light of different applications, especially in dynamical systems, where the Morse indices can be interpreted as homological indices of isolated invariant sets, and the connection matrix as a lower bound on the connecting orbits between them.
